PREFACE This new edition of First Course in the New Mathematics presents a vivid picture of the interest and utility which the new mathematics possesses for the seventh grade pupil. New, one-page unit previews, new illustrations, and up-to-date applications emphasize the vital, human values of the subject. 

The unit previews are an especially distinctive feature of this new edition. They are entitled KEY THOUGHTS and are designed to furnish stimulating surveys of the units they introduce. Their language is informal but purposely arresting in order to focus the pupil's attention on the highlights of each unit.

Each preview traces the thread of its unit's development. It stresses the practical, everyday utility of the mathematical principles and processes presented by the unit. In every way possible, it challenges the pupil to make direct, personal investigation and use of the principles discussed in the unit. 

The authors hope that the introduction of these previews will serve to emphasize anew the possibility of harmoniously blending practical procedures with progressive ideals. They have sought to achieve that happy mean in this book. At every turn they have utilized the social values of the subject to inculcate accurate understanding and encourage proficient application of its principles. Thrift, budgeting, investment, the mathematics of safety, and similar vital interests become real, living, personal issues whose importance makes drill for skill a necessary and altogether desirable outcome of the study. iii

iv 

PREFACE 

Special features appear in (1) Continual emphasis on the importance of estimating. 

(2) The plan for an Improvement Record Book in which the pupil constantly tests his speed and accuracy, of which he keeps a cumulative record. (3) The treatment of graphs to meet the need for visualizing ratios. (This makes the subject an integral part of the progressive development of the book.) (4) A large number of standard and other tests to help the pupil make frequent checks on his progress. (5) Constant but varied drill through practical applications of the facts and principles learned. (6) Simplicity and naturalness in discussion. (The text talks to the child in his own language.) (7) Interest-compelling headings, section titles, and picture captions. 

(8) Experimental problems which lead the pupil to discover for himself many of the major principles. (9) Varied pictures, many from pupil life, which vitalize the subject and brighten the book. 

The problems are practical and appropriate because they are based on everyday classroom needs. They are validated by the long teaching experience of the authors. 

Furthermore, literally thousands of papers have passed through the authors' hands, as examiners for the Regents of the State of New York, thus affording them an excellent cross-section of varying classroom conditions. 

In fine, the book aims to include and apply the seven cardinal features of this series: interest, simplicity, attractiveness, logical sequence, careful gradation, practical applications, and thoroughness. 

EDWARD I. EDGERTON PERRY A. CARPENTER

THE BEGINNINGS OF MATHEMATICS 

Have you ever watched or helped a little child get his first ideas of number? If so, you know how quickly he learns what one means, and two, and three, but then the difficulties begin. However, through the patience of his teachers and by his own practice and experience, he gradually gains skill in counting and measuring, and in time will learn much about fractions, aliquot parts, and all the other more complicated processes of mathematics. He rarely, if ever, asks why he needs to know these facts because there are so many illustrations in his own everyday life of their usefulness. 

He just takes the need of learning them for granted. 

When and how do you suppose the ideas of numbers and measure began for mankind? Probably they began when the human race, like the child, was very young. We know from study of primitive tribes and from what history tells us of ancient peoples that, when men first began to dwell together in small groups, there gradually developed an almost constant need of answering such questions as how many, how much, how far, how long. 

Naturally, the fingers were one of the first aids in counting, just as with a child, and 10 became the common base of number systems. The name digit which we use for any one of our ten Arabic numerals comes from the Latin word for finger and thus suggests this simplest way of representing numbers. In fact, whatever written records we have of the arithmetic of ancient peoples show that the symbols for 1, 2, 3, and 4 were always in the form of vertical or horizontal strokes as if representing the proper number of fingers. vii

viii 

THE BEGINNINGS OF MATHEMATICS 

Nevertheless, the complete number systems of the ancient world differed considerably, some being represented by use of an alphabet and others by various symbols. In spite of their differences, certain mechanical aids to reckoning were common to most systems. Thus the a'bacus, in one form or 

AN ABACUS 

another, one of the simplest and most accurate devices, was common to all, and is still widely used in eastern countries today.

The earliest written record we have of mathematics is a very old piece of writing called the Ahmes (Ah'mēs) Papyrus.

It was written about 1650 B.C. and was copied from a much older record. It shows that mathematics began to develop as a real science in Egypt as early as 3000 B.C. and that the arithmetic and geometry of the ancient Egyptians were well enough developed to meet the everyday needs of the people. Thus they could add and subtract whole numbers and certain fractions, and they had accurate though somewhat awkward methods of multiplying and dividing. They could measure off areas of land and apply other rules of geometry (earth measurement) to the construction of buildings, but beyond this knowledge they did not go.

In China, an earlier civilization and a broader interest in reasoning advanced the knowledge of mathematics far beyond the simple system of the Egyptians, but it did not spread to other countries. The Babylonians were great traders, and so their mathematics had largely to do with business and the stars. The work of the Hindus, carried to Europe by their

THE BEGINNINGS OF MATHEMATICS 

ix conquerors, the Arabs, gave us our present number system with its so-called Arabic numerals. 

Mathematics as we know it today really began with a famous Greek by the name of Tha'les of Miletus who lived from about 640 to 546 B.C. Pythag'oras, another famous mathematician, was said to have been a pupil of Thales. 

Under these two scholars, a large group of followers spread mathematical learning far and wide. When Alexandria became the center of learning in the third century before the birth of Christ, interested scholars were freed from other duties and supported by the government in their studies. 

One of the leading teachers at this center was Euclid, who systematized the whole of Greek mathematics. 

Once the Arabs had carried into Europe the knowledge of mathematics which they had acquired from the Hindus, the invention of the printing press and the publication of textbooks made certain the continued development of the subject. 

Modern mathematics is the basis of all modern invention. 

Without it we could not have airplanes, automobiles, controlled electricity, skyscrapers, and all the other conveniences of our machine age. All that the sciences of physics and chemistry have given us would be impossible without mathematics. Ships on the seas would lose their bearings if mathematics were not at hand to help, and business would return to the simple days of barter which your histories describe. 

No wonder, then, that mathematics is a subject which you must study to make a part of your knowledge. When some of your study seems a little hard, try to remember the thousands of years of progress behind it and the patient work of the famous men who discovered the laws of algebra and geometry, of trigonometry and calculus. Be grateful to them for the fine world which their efforts made possible and, who knows, perhaps you may reach the place where you can carry on their good work.

KEY THOUGHTS 

When you were very young, the things you prized because they belonged to you were always counted in whole numbers - twenty marbles, four dolls, two tops, three balls, one train, twelve books, twenty-four blocks. 

You weren't a bit interested in half a ball, or a quarter of a top, or a sixth of a doll! Your possessions had to be wholes, or they had no value.

A new ball meant one more ball added to those you already had; losing three marbles meant three marbles less in the marble bag. All you had you could count 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, until you reached the end.

Just so, a long time ago when the world was young, men left off their rude ways and settled down in little groups to till the soil and grow crops. 

- whole It was then that man began to need numbers numbers. How many days between the changes of the moon; how many days in the growing season; how long was a day; how many times should a man eat during the day; how many young did his animals bear; how many measures of grain did his fields produce? All these things, so important to each man, would be counted in whole numbers. 

Because you first thought of numbers as whole numbers, and just as early man first learned to handle whole numbers, let us make a beginning in this book with some practice in using whole numbers.

FIRST COURSE IN THE NEW MATHEMATICS PART I. THE NEW MATHEMATICS UNIT I . WHOLE NUMBERS 

Estimating Well. Here is a picture of the ocean liner 

Normandie, which is one of the largest ships afloat. 

FULL SPEED AHEAD! 

Courtesy The French Line 

How many inches wide do you think the picture is? 

How many inches high? Make a careful guess.

Attempting to tell the size or amount of anything, such as the width and height of this picture, is called estimating. 1

Everybody estimates, from the cook in the kitchen to the business man in the bank. If you train yourself to estimate well, you will save much time and trouble, and it will be hard to fool you with false figures. You will see at once the absurdity of your friend's statement when he says the ball field was a mile wide and there were about a million people at the game. 

Measuring. After you have made your estimate, you ought always to check it, that is, to find out how exact it is. To do this, you must know how to measure. 

Ability to measure accurately is even more important than estimating and is a help in nearly everything you do. 

Here is a ruler three inches long. Take a piece of paper or cardboard and make a ruler of your own six 1 2 3 

PART OF A RULER 

inches long. Now measure the picture and see how well you estimated.

Turn now to the pictures on pages 3 and 7. First estimate their dimensions and then measure them. accurate were your estimates?

How 

In these measurements, you had to use numbers. 

Without a thorough knowledge of numbers you cannot measure well. If your knowledge of numbers is to be thorough, it must rest upon a firm foundation.

The Four Walls of the Foundation. If a man builds a house one story high and later decides to add more stories, he ought first to strengthen the foundation. In your number work so far you have built up a pretty good knowledge of arithmetic. Now you are going to build upon that knowledge. Before you do so, you must strengthen the foundation. 99 66 "Foundation" and "fundamental" both come from the Latin word meaning "bottom. or base." The foundation of number work consists of the four fundamental or basic operations which are at the bottom of all mathematics. These are addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. 

They may be called the four walls of the foundation, and we shall begin by strengthening them before building up our knowledge of the new mathematics.

1. Mr. Wood agreed to pay for Jack's lunches in the cafeteria at school if he would make a written statement at the end of each week as to how much they cost. During his first week at school they cost as follows: Monday $.24; Tuesday $.18; Wednesday $.20; Thursday $.28;

Friday $.25. Read these numbers over again and find the total in your head as you go along. Now add them up on paper and see how much Jack's father paid him. 

Was your answer right when you worked it in your head? 

2. Mr. Wood took Jack and some friends on a ten-day motor trip. The first day they drove 173 miles; the second, 207; the third, 194; and the others, 238, 189, 219, 193, 179, 192, 187. What was the total mileage? 

3. The junior high school had a school savings bank, and Jack's father told him that he would give him an allowance for his lunches if Jack would start a savings account and put something into it each month. Throughout the year Jack was able to earn money by doing odd jobs, and a large part of this he saved and put into the bank. May; and $3.10 in June, how much did he have in the savings bank at the end of the school year? Why was

How We Find the Median Score. -a. If a class of 

21 pupils in a given test have the following scores: 

6, 6, 7, 7, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 10, 10, Median score the eleventh score, or 8, is the median score. b. If a class of 20 pupils in a given test have these scores: 

3, 4, 5, 6, 6, 6, 7, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 9, 9, 9, 9, 10, 10, 10, 10 = Median score the median score is halfway between the tenth score and the eleventh score, or 8 in this case. c. A third class of 18 pupils took a test and obtained these scores : 

4, 6, 6, 7, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 9, 10, 10. 

Median score

The median score is halfway between the two middle scores, or 8. As the tests will not use fractional marks, we shall call 9 the median score in this test.

PRACTICING WITH MEDIANS 

1. In a how many pupils were above the median? 

2. What is the lowest score in a? 

3. In b how many pupils had a score equal to the median score? 

4. How many had a score of 9 in c? 

5. Try to find the median of some of your tests. 

6. In which half of the class will you be if your score is above the median? 

7. If all the pupils work hard to stand high, what is the probable effect upon the median ?

IMPROVEMENT RECORD BOOK 

When you play tennis or basket ball, you always try to improve your skill. When you run races, you keep trying for a better record. In this book you will find tests to try your skill in mathematics. In some cases the standard or average time for working the test is given; in others it is to be determined by your teacher or by a committee of your classmates. If you compare your records with these averages, you can be constantly measuring your skill.

To keep these records, you are to get a notebook, to be called your Improvement Record Book, which will be referred to hereafter by the initials "I. R. B." Write your name, school, and class on it. The tests for which records are to be entered in the 

I. R. B. are indicated by black type with a numeral in brackets, like this: I. R. B. Test [1]. 

To record your improvement, you are to take each test four times. Directions are given in the text. The following chart is suggested for your scores:

How much change would she have brought back if her mother had given her only $2.75? Estimate your change as Ruth did. Then check your estimate on paper. 

The Third Wall: Multiplication. As you go on in this book, you will see that everything is planned to help you work better and faster. Multiplication is a quick way to add. You can often do it in your head. 

"DO-IT-IN-YOUR-HEAD" OR MENTAL ARITHMETIC 

Did you ever hear folks say, "Do-it-in-your-head "? 

Business men sometimes advise their clerks, "Do not waste time with pencil and paper, use your head." Time is money. Neither business men nor you can afford to waste either. Doing-it-in-your-head saves time and money.

There are many simple exercises and examples in this book. Always try first to work them in your head. 

You will be surprised how quickly your ability to work mentally will grow and how much time you will save.

THE THIRD WALL: MULTIPLICATION 

17 

GAMES AND CONTESTS IN MENTAL ARITHMETIC¹ 

You and your classmates, with your teacher's help, can devise many games or contests in Mental Arithmetic. For instance, in example 1 of the following exercise your teacher might call for volunteers to join an Oral Contest, dividing the volunteers into two teams, and appointing a captain for each team. 

Let the first team take the upper row of multiplications and the second team the lower row. Your teacher, alternating between teams, will dictate the examples. In case of a mistake, the multiplication should go to the next pupil of the opposing team; in case this pupil misses, back to the next pupil in order of the first team and so on. Each captain should score one for every correct answer of his side. 

If some of your classmates are not ready to volunteer, your teacher might make a Pencil-Paper team for these pupils. This third team should work individually at their seats. 

When you have finished let the Pencil-Paper pupils be divided between the two Oral teams; then all join in an oral contest of repeating the Multiplication Tables 1 to 12. Let each pupil have one trial on some one table. Score five for each whole table correctly given.

Finally, have team captains add the total scores to decide the winning team. 

1 The games and contests of this and all succeeding units are “boxed”; that is, they are surrounded by a black line. They are optional with your teacher and classmates. Your teacher will decide whether or not class time will permit their use. It may be decided to follow some suggestions, to omit others, or to devise your own plans. To gain the time needed for a class contest, you and your classmates might volunteer to do part of the lesson's written work outside class time.

THE FOURTH WALL: DIVISION 

23 

The Fourth Wall: Division. - Division requires the assistance of addition, subtraction, and multiplication. 

It is the opposite of multiplication. Thus if 9 times 7 equals 63, then 63 divided by 7 gives us 9. 63 in this example is called the dividend, 7 is called the divisor, and 

9 is the quotient.

Again, if we divide 38 by 9, we obtain 4 as a quotient with 2 left over. This is called the remainder. The remainder is usually written as the numerator of a fraction of which the divisor is the denominator. 38 ÷ 9 

You may check your work in two ways: 

1. By repeating the work. 43. 

2. By multiplying the divisor by the quotient and adding the remainder, thus obtaining the dividend.

Two-TEAM CONTEST¹ 

Plan a Team Contest for the preceding example 18. Again let your teacher divide the class into two teams and appoint a captain for each team. While your teacher dictates the examples each team member should write the answers on paper. When the work is finished the team captains will collect the papers and exchange them; then distribute the opponents' papers to their own team members. While your teacher dictates the answers let each pupil check the paper distributed by the captain; then score the paper by allowing one credit for each correct addition, subtraction, multiplication, or division. Have the captains total the credits of their own teams. The greater total will determine the winning team.

Finding an Average. Most of us know what an average is, though we may not be able to define it. We are going to use mathematics to find averages. 

What was the average amount that Jack Wood paid for his lunch (page 6)? To find the answer we add the amounts paid for the lunch for each of the five days and divide by 5.

KEY THOUGHTS 

It must have been quite a while ago that you first heard the word "divide," meaning to break into parts. Perhaps it was on a day when you were out in your yard eating an orange and Johnny, from next door, arrived. "Give Johnny a piece of your orange," called out your mother. "Divide with him. That's a good boy." 

One that whole, big one - had to be divided! Perhaps into two or three or four equal parts; perhaps into a small and a big part. But divided somehow it was; changed into smaller bits that, added together, made one. 

In some such way the ancients found out about parts of what they had always thought of as wholes. Perhaps a man had to divide a measure of grain; perhaps he had to pay for part of a day's work; perhaps he discovered a year was four seasons long, and a season was therefore a quarter of a year. 

Now it is plain to see that a division of a whole may be a whole in itself like one spring, one fall; one day, one night. Each is a whole, but each is also a part of a bigger whole. You all know that fifty cents is a pretty good whole by itself, but it is nevertheless a half of a bigger whole. 

Thus a division, or a part, or a fraction of a whole is always thought of in relation to something bigger. 

Let's go on and see what we can do with these fractions of wholes. 30

UNIT II BREAKING UP WHOLE NUMBERS INTO FRACTIONS FOR FINE WORK 

Fractions. A football game is divided into halves, and each half is again divided into two equal parts. 

What are these parts called? 

Hockey is usually played in three twenty-minute periods. What part of the hockey game is each period? 

How many innings are there in a baseball game? 

What part of a regular baseball game is an inning?

Fractions are nothing new; they are a continuation of division which we have just studied. But the beauty of fractions is that they help us to do easily so many things which without them would be very difficult. 

Use of Fractions. When you motor over a highway that is being repaired, you often see piles of rock and other materials gathered near a stone crusher. They are to be broken up into finer material to build the smooth road which makes driving swift and pleasant. 

In just this way in mathematics, the whole number one is broken into smaller parts, called fractions, to enable us to do finer and quicker work. It will pay us to learn the fine points in mathematics; this we cannot do without a knowledge of fractions.

In making a dress or a suit, fractions may be used, as for example 3 yards of cloth. When you purchase meat, your butcher may weigh 21 pounds for you. 

On page 50, you will find fractions used in recipes.

Gasoline is also sold at a price that uses fractions. 31

BREAKING UP WHOLE NUMBERS INTO FRACTIONS 

Different Kinds of Fractions. We must learn to recognize and name several kinds of fractions. 

Common fractions have two parts, the numerator (the number above the line) and the denominator (the number below the line). These are called the terms of the fraction.

The denominator shows into how many parts the number is divided and the numerator shows how many of these parts are taken. For example, in this figure AC is a figure one inch long 

The shaded

Ꭰ B C divided into 4 equal parts. part AB shows 3 of those 4 equal parts. of AC. AD or of AC. Thus AB is The fraction may also be thought of as a short way of indicating 3 ÷ 4.

A proper fraction is a common fraction whose numerator is smaller than its denominator, as §, 1, .

An improper fraction is one whose numerator is equal to or larger than its denominator, as §, §. 

A mixed number is a whole number (called integer) plus a fraction. Thus, 6+ 61. 

20 24/7 

Changing the Forms of Fractions. Fractions which have different numerators and different denominators may still be equivalent in value. For instance, for one half dollar has the same value as two quarters.

Fractions may be changed in form, but not in value, by either multiplying or dividing both numerator and denominator by the same number.

Illustrative Example: 

12 21 1 X 2 2 X 2 2÷2 4 ÷ 2 214 12 2 20 1 X 3 3 2 X 3 4 ÷ 4 20 ÷ 4 115

LOWEST TERMS 

35 

Lowest Terms. A fraction is said to be reduced to its lowest terms when both numerator and denominator are as small as they can be made without changing the value of the fraction. This is done by dividing both terms by their greatest common divisor. Always reduce fractions to their lowest terms before performing any operations. This makes your work easy. For instance, 1+18 looks hard, but when reduced to its lowest terms it becomes easy: +1.

MULTIPLYING FRACTIONS 

41 

Multiplying Fractions. (1) To multiply a fraction by a whole number (integer): Divide the denominator by the integer if the result will be a whole number; if not, multiply the numerator by the integer. (2) To multiply two or more fractions, first divide any numerator and any denominator by the largest number which is contained in both. (This is sometimes called canceling.) Then multiply all numerators for a new numerator and all denominators for a new denominator. If the dividing has been properly done, the answer will be in its lowest terms.

BREAKING UP WHOLE NUMBERS INTO FRACTIONS COMMON SENSE CAN CONVINCE YOU 

Estimating is of great value in multiplication. It means applying your common sense. For instance, in example 1 below, if you estimate that 21 X 144 is 28, your common sense tells you that your estimate is absurd because not only is 2 X 14 equal to 28 but also of 14 is 7, which added to 28 is 35; certainly then your answer is over 35.

Estimating prevents absurd answers. In the following table, one answer is absurd and one is correct. First estimate, then check the correct answers as rapidly as you can. 1. 2 X 14 28 361 2. X 400 266 300 6. 816 612 7. X 400 1/4/1 76 3. 100 X 624 75 8. 21 X6 1423 126 4.

TWO HEADS ARE BETTER THAN ONE 

Mathematics "comes naturally," as the saying is, to some of you, but to some others it " comes hard." This gives your class a fine opportunity for some good team work. 

Those of you who are getting pretty good marks can help boost the class average by first getting your own work out of the way and then helping out some classmates who need help. Such team work will produce splendid results. 

You can plan your own way of working together. Perhaps your teacher will make the assignments of couples to work together and will let you know which pair of pupils gets the best results from the scheme. Then the rest of the class can, in future work of the same sort, follow the plan of the winning couple. If the teacher wishes, you may try the following test by couples.

BREAKING UP WHOLE NUMBERS INTO FRACTIONS 

Aliquot Parts. An aliquot part is a part which divides a whole number evenly. Thus 10, 12, 16, 20, 25, 33, and 50 are all aliquot parts of 100, for they divide it evenly into 10, 8, 6, 5, 4, 3, and 2 parts, respectively; that is, they are respectively,,, 1, 1, 1, and of 100.

It is often convenient to use these fractions instead of the corresponding aliquot parts. This is shown in the 

Illustrative Example: What will 16 pounds of confectioner's sugar cost at 12 cents a pound? 

Since one pound costs 12 cents, or of $1, 16 pounds would cost 16 X $, or $2.

See how much easier it is to work the example this way than to multiply 16 X 12 cents.

REVIEW TRIAL OF I. R. B. TESTS OF UNIT I 

Try to improve your 

B. tests in Unit I,

On the page where 

Do not take the class time for this exercise. scores all by yourself. Try again the four I. R. which you have already recorded in your I. R. B. you entered your scores for the first and second trials make a record of the third. If not satisfied, try until you have improved your scores. 

Do not think that you must always be watched in taking these tests. A junior high school pupil is certainly old enough to be trusted. 

If you falsify your records, you cheat only yourself. The sensible thing is to go after a better record, as you would do in athletics.

Remember also that anything which brings discredit upon you brings even greater disgrace to your class and to your school. Neither life in-school nor life out-of-school has a place for a cheat. So after all, one great test which you are making in these I. R. B. tests is the trial of your honor. You will be a worthy junior high school boy or girl if you always pass this test with a perfect record.

COACHING COUPLES AND COACHING COMMITTEE ¹ 

OR 

"C. C. AND C. C. GAME” 

Before starting the next two exercises, "Recipe Problems" and "Store Problems," each pupil should work independently six original examples in fractions which your teacher places on the blackboard. Give each pupil 163 credits for each correct answer. Let the three pupils who first secure 100% records be a 

Coaching Committee to assist the teacher.

Divide the rest of the class evenly according to their scores. 

Let each pupil in the high-score half of the class work with some one from the low-score half who wants his help; each such pair of pupils will compose a Coaching Couple. 

The high-score pupil of each Coaching Couple should let the low-score pupil work the examples of the following two exercises, assisting only when necessary. If any couple needs assistance, the Coaching Committee and your teacher will help.

In "C. C. and C. C. Games" your class may decide to take one class period, or a part of one, for the work of the game. Then, after getting this start, the Coaching Couples may agree upon a time and place, either in school or at home, to complete the work.

A GARDENING PROJECT 

Edward Towner was a member of a boy scout troop in his junior high school. The Scoutmaster told the troop that each boy who could make money by helping some one else would receive a merit badge. Edward's father, who was a farmer and gardener, learned of this offer and decided to give Edward a plot of ground for a garden.

1. Edward's father told him that he might have all his profits for his bank account. Edward decided to set out strawberry plants on one half of his garden and devote the rest to early vegetables. One third of this latter portion of the garden was planted with peas, one half with onions, and the rest with lettuce. What part of the garden was planted with peas?

X = ? 

Does of mean the same as X? What part of the garden was planted with onions? 

X? See if you can find exactly what part of the garden was planted with lettuce. 

2. Strawberries do not begin to bear until the second year. At the end of that time he had sold 

156 quarts of berries at an average price of 12

EDWARD HOEING HIS GARDEN 

cents a quart, 320 heads of lettuce at 5 cents each, 210 bunches of young onions at 8 cents a bunch, and 4 bushels (how many quarts in a bushel?) of green peas at 15 cents a quart. What was his income from his garden in these two years? 

3. Edward's expenses for the two years were as follows:

For purchasing tools $5; for fertilizer $5 each year; for lime $2; for strawberry plants $6; for seed 50 cents. 

What amount did he make for his bank account?

Last year Mr. Towner sowed 40 acres to wheat, which yielded 35 bushels to the acre; 10 acres to oats, which yielded 50 bushels to the acre; and planted 12 acres to potatoes, which yielded 160 bushels to the acre. How many bushels of grain did he raise? The remainder of the farm was in hay and he cut on the average 1 ton of hay to the acre. How many tons of hay did he cut? 

What part of his farm was in hay?

END-OF-UNIT TRIAL OF I. R. B. TESTS 

In this unit you have seen whole numbers broken up into fractions for finer work. You have reviewed different kinds of fractions and have learned that the four fundamental processes are used with fractions as well as with whole numbers. 

You have also learned coöperation (working together) as illustrated in the "C. C. and C. C. Games." Teamwork always makes work better and more enjoyable.

Then, too, you have been given a chance to make a clean record of honor and trustworthiness. You alone know whether or not you passed that test with a 100% record. If you know that you did, never permit that record to be lowered. 

Now turn back through the pages of Unit II and find the tests for which you entered records in your Improvement Record Book. 

Have your class decide how you will take the second trials of these five tests, whether in class, in school, or at home. 

Compare your present End-of-Unit records with the First Trial records to see if you are improving the I. R. B. Enter your new time and accuracy scores in the column, End-of-Unit Trial.

KEY THOUGHTS 

Have you ever heard the word "graphic"? Do you know what it means?

When we say a thing is pictured graphically, or that a painting is a graphic reproduction, we mean that it is vivid and lifelike.

When you hear someone talk about a graphic record, he is probably talking about a record that has been kept in some clear, quickly understood way.

There is a way of keeping records and showing relationships and progress and change in a very graphic fashion. 

That way is by means of graphs. Graphs are diagrams that show at a glance how one quantity depends on or changes with another.

It so happens that quite often in mathematics we wish to show relationships in a vivid way, and this can be done by means of very interesting graphs. 

For instance, do you know the relation between the number of hours a day you sleep and the number of hours you go to school? Do you know the relation between the games your baseball team won last spring and those they lost? 

Did you ever wonder how you could show the relation between the allowance you receive and the amount you spend each week?

All these, and lots of other problems, can be shown by making graphs.

There is much value in learning about this way of showing relationships, and it can be made as interesting as a game. 

Let's give it a try! 62

UNIT III FRACTIONS AS RATIOS PICTURED IN GRAPHS 

In the last unit we thought of a fraction as a division to indicate part of a number. We are now going to study fractions as ratios to indicate the relation of two numbers, that is, a comparison between them. 

MARY BROWN AND HER BROTHERS Fractions as Number Relations. - Mary Brown is 

12 years old. She has two brothers, John who is 4 and 

Horace who is 9. Mr. Wood asked Mary how her age compared with John's and she said, "I am three times as old as John." "Then," said Mr. Wood, "John is a third as old as you are." Mary then said: “I am as old as Horace, for of 9 is 12." "Right," said Mr. Wood, "that means Horace is as old as you." 63

FRACTIONS AS RATIOS PICTURED IN GRAPHS 

Ratio. A ratio is the quotient of two like numbers. 

This quotient may be expressed in several ways but is most frequently written as a fraction, usually reduced to its lowest terms. To find the ratio of one number to another, divide the first by the second. 

The ratio of Mary's age to John's age is three to one, † or 3. 

The ratio of John's age to Mary's age is one to three, (read"1 is to 3 ").

The ratio of Mary's age to Horace's is four to three, (read " 4 is to 3").

The ratio of Horace's age to Mary's is three to four, (read "3 is to 4"). 

The ratio of Horace's age to John's age is? 

The ratio of John's age to Horace's age is?

BAR-GRAPH PICTURES 

67 For making graphs you will need graph paper, often called " squared paper." You will find some in the pocket on the back cover of this book, but for your work in graphs you must get several large sheets. 

Notice that there are squares of two sizes. How many small squares long is one large square? The side of a small square is what part of the side of a large square? 

If the side of a large square is called 1, what would the side of a small square be called?

There are many different kinds of graph paper, but we shall use only two in this book. One has 100 small squares in each large square; the other has only 25. 

We shall generally use the former. 

Bar-Graph Pictures. The first graph we are going to study is called a bar graph. It is illustrated by the following example:

In Mary Thompson's class in arithmetic there are 25 boys and girls. In her class in nature study there Arithmetic

Nature Study

History 

GRAPH OF MARY THOMPSON'S CLASSES 

are 20. In her class in English there are 30, and in her history class there are 32. The comparative size of these classes is shown by these heavy black lines called bars. 

These show the ratio of each class to every other class. 

A bar exactly as long as one large square represents 10. 

How many pupils does a bar as long as 1 small square represent? The first bar is as long as 2 large squares, and thus represents 2 × 10, or 25 pupils. The second represents 20, the third 30, and the fourth 32.

Making Bar Graphs. -Bar graphs are very useful to represent large numbers. In such cases they are not supposed to be absolutely accurate but approximations.

Illustrative Example: In a certain city the cost of the public schools was $125,350; police $60,100; fire department $45,800; streets and parks $52,600; public health $18,250. Picture the reiation of these costs by the use of bar graphs.

MAKING BAR GRAPHS 

69 Draw a heavy vertical line near the left side of your graph sheet, and make all the bars start evenly at that line. The longest bar must represent $125,350. The longest that we can make it with the paper that we are using is 9 large squares. Each large Schools Police Fire Streets Health 15 30 45 60 75 90

square then must represent at least of $125,350, which is nearly $14,000. Let us then make one large square represent $15,000 and then each small square here will represent $3000 if we use paper like that above that has 25 small squares in one large square. $125,350 ÷ 15,000 = 8 large squares, with $5350 remaining. $53503000 1.78+ small squares. Hence the bar, which represents $125,350, has a length equal to 8 large squares and a little more than 12 small squares. In a similar way we divide $60,100 by 15,000 to find the bar representing police expenses; and so on for the other departments. 

Make a similar one of your city.

Pictures of Ratios by the Broken-Line Graph. 

Another kind of graph which is very often used is the broken-line graph.

Illustrative Example: 

In June, Jerome Thomas had $24 in the savings bank. In 

July he had $39, in August $50. Then he bought a suit of clothes that cost $27, so that he had only $23 in the bank in September. 

The following month his uncle gave him $50 for his birthday. 

He spent $5 of this and put $45 in the bank, so that in October he had $68 in the bank. In November he had $80. Make a graph showing the amounts that he had in the bank each month. Dollars 90 

GRAPH OF JEROME THOMAS'S SAVINGS 

Draw a heavy black line down the left side of the paper. 

Across the bottom draw another which meets the first line so as to make an exactly square corner. These lines are called axes, and the point where they meet is called the origin. Mark this point 0. Then on the horizontal (see dictionary) axis mark off the months in order on the heavy lines as shown here, beginning with the vertical axis under which we write June. On the vertical axis mark off amounts of money, letting each large square equal $30. Then each small square will represent of $30, or $6. Oct. Nov.

FRACTIONS AS RATIOS PICTURED IN GRAPHS 

Now Jerome had $24 in the bank in June, so we mark the point above June at a position to represent $24. (It may help you to think of this point as the corner of $24 Street and June Avenue.) 

This would be 4 small squares up for $24. In the same manner on the line marked July (or you can think of it as July Avenue) we locate and mark the point to represent $39, and so on for the other months. When these points are all located and marked, we connect them with a broken line, as shown in the diagram above. 

This graph gives us a fine idea of the way in which Jerome's bank account grew.

Do you think that he ought to try to keep the line always going upward? Why?

If it were your bank account, would you want the broken line to be steep or more nearly horizontal? Explain why.

LEARNING ABOUT THE WEATHER 

Among the instruments used by the weather man in making his reports and predictions are the thermometer, the barometer, the rain gauge, and an instrument to measure the velocity of the wind. 

THE WEATHER BUREAU AT WASHINGTON 

Electrically recording instruments are connected with apparatus on the roof and give a continuous record of weather conditions. 

There are several kinds of thermometers. The one you have in your living room or outside on the porch is probably a Fahrenheit thermometer. On this kind of thermometer the freezing point of water is marked 32°, and the boiling point of water is marked 212°. (The sign that stands for the word degree is °.) 

1. How many degrees are there between the freezing point and the boiling point?

FRACTIONS AS RATIOS PICTURED IN GRAPHS 

2. The accompanying graph shows the average temperature by months for St. Paul, Minnesota. Which is the coldest month? the warmest? What is the average 

Degrees F. 80 70 60 50 40 30 20 20 10 

AVERAGE TEMPERATURES IN ST. PAUL, MINN. 

annual range of temperature (difference between highest and lowest)? Which month had an average temperature just freezing? Which months had average temperatures below freezing?

3. In the northern part of North America the temperature in winter often falls below zero. One day the temperature in Winnipeg, Canada, was reported as 16 degrees below zero (often written -16°), and in 

Jacksonville, Florida, 68°. What was the difference in temperature between these cities? 

4. The lowest temperature ever recorded in New York city was 13 degrees below zero (-13°) on December 23, 1891, and the highest was 102° on August 24, 1893. 

What was the difference between those temperatures? 

5. The clinical thermometer is a special form of a Fahrenheit thermometer used to measure the body temperatures of people. The average temperature of healthy people is 98.6°. This is called normal. When one's temperature is higher than normal, he has fever. Once when Jane Bradley was very ill, her temperature was 

105.2°. How many degrees of fever did she have? 

6. Men engaged in scientific work often use a Centigrade thermometer. Look in the dictionary or encyclopedia and find out where the freezing and boiling points of water are marked on this thermometer. 

The mercury in a barometer measures air pressure. In fair weather at sea level the mercury in the barometer stands about 29.9 inches high. In higher altitudes the mercury column is shorter, the average decline being 1.08 inches for each 1000 feet of elevation. What should be the height of the mercury in fair weather on the top of Mt. McKinley (20,300 feet elevation)? Estimate the height of a mountain on which the barometer registered 

21.4 inches in fair weather.

A sudden drop in the barometer means that a storm is approaching.

Graphs and Ratio in Estimating. - Graphs are a great help in estimating. They help us see things at a glance. 

The ratio of the estimate to the exact answer should be very close. Business men often use graphs in estimating. 

Mr. Hawley, a merchant, gave this problem to his three sons, Alfred, Clarence, and Fred: "A man bought 

8 yards of cloth for $3.75. How much could he get for $15?" Alfred obtained as an answer 43 yards. Clarence obtained 30 yards and Fred 25. Mr. Hawley did not wish to take the trouble to work this out with pencil and paper, so he made a mental estimate like this: "$3.75 is a little less than $4, and $15 is a little less than four times 4, so for $15 one could get about four times 8 yards, or about 34.". He concluded that Clarence was nearest the right answer. Do you agree?

On another occasion the boys tried to divide 36.72 by 

6.8. Alfred obtained 3.6, Clarence 5.4, and Fred 7.8. 

Mr. Hawley saw at once that 36 ÷ 7 = 5. Since the divisor is a little less than 7, the quotient would be a little more than 54, therefore he decided that Clarence was right.

A GRAPH GAME¹ 

Have a committee appointed from your class to gather facts which can be pictured by bar, rectangle, or broken-line graphs. 

Appoint two members of the class to act with your teacher as judges. Ask members of the class to prepare graphs of the facts gathered and present these graphs to the judges. Let the winner be known as the "Champion Graph Artist" and let him keep a graph record on the blackboard of the class attendance for a week. If there is more than one class studying graphs, a second contest may be held between the champions.

Since the airplane industry has grown with such rapidity in the past few years and may grow much faster in the years to come, you should familiarize yourself with many things that go to make up this great modern method of transportation. 

See if you can tell the missing words: tons,

You might be interested to know that a certain Douglas airplane weighs with full load 17,500 pounds or and when empty, 11,780 pounds or tons. 

It will carry a passenger load of 2040 pounds or about people averaging 170 pounds each. Its maximum speed of 210 miles an hour at 8000 feet is about as fast as the fastest train. It may rise to about 23,000 feet or about miles. Its total rated power is 1420 horsepower or about as much as a good eight-cylinder automobile. 

The wing area is about 940 square feet or about square yards. The length is about 60 feet or 

Its height is 16 feet or yards.

KEY THOUGHTS 

What shape is your baseball diamond? 

How was it measured off? 

How big is it?

These questions, and many more like them about "how big," can be answered in a language of size called geometry.

Long ago men first began to measure surfaces not for baseball diamonds or football fields, or for cellars or for roads, but because they wanted to know how much seed they could scatter on or reap from a field, or how many animals could graze on their pasture lands. 

Later on, walls had to be built to protect a man's flocks or gardens; houses were built in which families might live; temples were erected in which men might worship their gods. 

Here were problems in construction that had to do with determining a point, an angle, and then drawing a line. The point is where a line begins or ends, and the angle determines in which direction the line will go. There are certain truths about these relationships that had to be discovered. 

If you stop to think a moment you will be amazed to discover in how many ways you use this language of size without knowing about geometry at all. 

When you take a short cut across a field you are making use of one of the truths of geometry. Do you know what it is?

Your fishing line from the tip of your pole to the hook hidden in the water is a straight line. Do you know why? When you turn down a side street from the main thoroughfare you are following a geometric pattern. Do you know what it is?

It is proper for us to find out about this thing called geometry, since it is so unavoidably a part of our everyday life. 108

UNIT IV PICTURES OF  ¹ 

Our study of the last unit brings us to a still more interesting part of the New Mathematics. This is the use of lines in measuring; it is called geometry. 

What Geometry Is. This name geometry comes to us from two old Greek words meaning earth measurement. We all make almost daily use of "earth measurement." Geometry is one of the most useful branches of mathematics. 

Lines are the first tools of geometry. Every figure, every diagram starts with a line. You have used lines all your life. So without being aware of it you have always used geometry. Now you are going to learn what some of the other tools of geometry are and how to use them in solving problems in mathematics. 

The Straight Line. You all know what a straight line is even though it is hard to define. In the country when a boy wants to go swimming, he usually goes "straight across lots" to the swimming hole instead of going around by the road. This is because a straight line is the shortest path between two points. Do you sometimes "cut corners" in the city? What is meant by "jaywalking"? Why do people do it? 

The word "straight" comes from an old word meaning "stretched." A stretched wire is a good illustration of a straight line. If you drive a nail in the corner of your house and another in the corner of your garage, there can be only one straight line connecting them. Hence, we say that two points determine a straight line. Since two straight 

1 TEACHER'S NOTE: Units IV to VI may be supplemented, if desired, by the additional work in geometry on pages 440–444.

109

lines cannot join the same two points, it follows that two straight lines intersect in only one point. If 

How many "sights" are there on a gun barrel? you sight along the gun barrel until the target is "in line," what does that mean?

A line like the line of sight we have just mentioned is of unlimited length. When we mean a limited line like one joining two points, we call it a line segment, or a sect. 

How Line Segments Are Named. — 

The line segment which joins the points A and B is usually called simply “the line AB" or "the line BA."

Sometimes a single letter is used to indicate a line segment. In the accompanying diagram we have the lines a, b, and c meeting at a point. These are called rays. 

Make a dot or

Old and New Means of Measuring. 

point on your paper and put the letter A beside it. We shall use this letter to represent the name of the point, and call it "the point A." A short distance away make another point, B. Draw a straight line segment between 

A and B. Can you do it freehand? If not, use a ruler.

A ruler is usually a bar of wood or celluloid having a straight edge. For this reason it is often called a straightedge. The diagram shows a part of a foot ruler. Inches Centimeters

For what other purpose is a ruler used besides drawing straight-line segments?

ESTIMATING AND MEASURING LENGTHS OF LINES 111 

What part of an inch is the smallest division on the upper scale? What part of an inch is each of the other divisions? How many inches in a foot? How many feet in a yard? How many feet in a rod? How many 

If you cannot answer these questions, learn the table of linear measure in the back of this textbook. feet in a mile?

Now look at the lower edge of the ruler. It has different divisions from those of the upper edge. The large divisions are called centimeters and the smaller divisions are called millimeters. Look at it carefully and tell how many millimeters make a centimeter. This method of measuring distances is called the metric system. It is used throughout a large part of the world and is coming to be used more and more in the United States. Learn the table of metric linear measure in the back of this textbook. 

What metric unit is nearest to a yard? nearest to a mile?

Estimating and Measuring Lengths of Lines. — Using a folded sheet of paper as a straightedge, draw a line that looks three inches long. Measure it with your ruler.

When you measure a line, be sure to place the ruler so that the very end of an inch or centimeter space falls exactly on the end of the line to be measured. It is not possible to measure exactly with ordinary instruments, but you should be able to measure a short line to the nearest sixteenth of an inch or to the nearest millimeter. 

Practice this until you can. 

How long is the line that you drew? How much was your error? Try it again. Did you do better this time?

First estimate and then measure the length and width of your book by both linear and metric measures. 

Do the

MEASURING LENGTH 

same for the top of your desk. 

The picture on this page shows 

Mary Kiernan measuring the length of a crayon box. How long is the crayon box in your room?

Measuring Longer Lines. 

When we want to measure a long line like the length of a street or the distance between two towns, we get surveyors or civil engineers to measure it. Surveyors use in place of a ruler a steel tape 100 feet long, divided into feet and tenths of a foot. An instrument called a transit is used to measure angles. With these instruments, it is possible to measure a city block to the nearest foot. 

Ewing Galloway

THE CIRCLE 

113 Broken and Curved Lines. In the chapter on graphs we had pictures of broken lines but we did not define them. A line made up of several line segments running in different directions is called a broken line. 

A curved line or curve is a line no part of which is straight. 

The Circle. The most important curved line is called a circle. It is a closed curve which is everywhere the same distance from a point inside called the center. Can you draw a good circle free-hand? Try it. Now try it again, using a circular object such as a coin. Name other circular objects with which you might draw circles. Could you draw a circle of any size that you might want?

COMPASSES 

115 The distance from any point on a circle to its center is called the radius. To draw a circle with a radius of one inch, open the compasses so that the distance between the end points is one inch. Then thrust the sharp point into the paper and turn the pencil point completely around, forming a closed curve. This is a circle. 

The point where the sharp end pierced is its center. the paper

Radius Center Arc Any part of the curve of the circle is called an arc. 

Many arches are arcs of circles. All lines made by the compasses are arcs, when they stop short of a complete circle.

AB. If a chord passes through the center, it is a diameter; as the line

Ewing Galloway

Angles. As the hands of a clock rotate, they form various two-sided figures, called angles. In the same way, if a radius turns (rotates) about the center of its circle, it makes different angles with other radii of the circle. In the figure, angle ABC shows how much the radius AB must turn about point B to reach the position of CB. The radii AB and CB are called the sides of the angle and point B is its vertex. -A As we cannot conveniently picture an angle as the rotation of a radius, we may represent it by drawing two radii of a circle. The angle is measured by the amount of rotation or opening between the two radii. Since all the radii of a circle are drawn from the same point, we may say, then, that an angle is the figure formed when two straight lines are drawn from the same point. In naming an angle we usually place a letter at the vertex, and one at the other end of each side, as shown in the diagram. 

When only one angle is named at a point, we may identify it simply by the vertex letter. Thus this angle may be called "the angle B."

Kinds of Angles. When one line meets another, as in the diagram, so that the two angles formed are equal, each is called a right angle. A right angle is often called a square corner. How many right angles can you see from where you sit?

When two lines meet in such a cway that they form a right angle, Right Right Angle Angle each line is said to be perpendicular to the other. Thus, the line AB is perpendicular to the line CD, and the line CD is perpendicular to the line AB.

KINDS OF ANGLES 

117 A straight angle is an angle whose sides lie in the same straight line but extend in opposite directions. Thus, two radii which form a diameter make a straight angle.

-A An acute angle is an angle smaller than a right angle. Acute is another word for sharp.

An obtuse angle is an angle larger than a right angle and smaller than two right angles.

Obtuse

Angle 

Obtuse is another word for blunt.

Two lines are oblique to each other when they make either an acute or an obtuse -D angle. All angles except straight angles and right angles are oblique angles. 

Two angles are complementary if their sum equals a right angle, as angles BCD and ACD or angles 1 and 2.

B- 

Two angles are supplementary if their 2 -E sum equals a straight angle, as angles 

ECF and BCF or angles 3 and 4.

Two complementary angles are called complements of each other, and two supplementary angles, supplements. Two right angles form a straight angle when they are placed side by side. When two obtuse angles are placed side by side, they form an angle larger than a straight angle, called a reflex angle. 

When a line makes a complete rotation, a round angle has been formed. A round angle is equal to two straight angles, or four right angles.

How Angles Are Measured. - Angles are measured by degrees. A degree is of a right angle.

Here is a right angle divided into degrees. The small division along OA is an angle of 1 degree. 

Four right angles placed together like this take up all the space around Athe point O. Hence the sum of all the angles that can be drawn around a point is 360 degrees.

21 -B 10 180° 90° 60° -30° -20° -10° -0° 270° If we draw a circle with O for a center, the circle measures the sum of all the angles that can be drawn around

HOW ANGLES ARE MEASURED 

121 the point O. So we say that the circle has 360 degrees. 

The sign that stands for the word degree is °, thus 360°. 

One degree equals 60 minutes (60′) and one minute equals 

60 seconds (60'). 

Learn the table of angular measure in the back of the book.

The Protractor is an instrument used to measure angles. You will 90 80 60 70 110 100 90 ST OST OFT 100 10 190 80 20 00 

A PROTRACTOR 

130 140 find one in a pocket attached to the back cover of this book. Usually protractors are made of brass, celluloid, or heavy cardboard, and are in the shape of half a circle (semicircle).

To measure the angle CAD we place the protractor so that the little point in the middle of its straight edge is at the vertex A, and this edge falls along the side AD of the angle. 

Where the other side of the angle (side AC) or an extension of it cuts. the circular edge of the protractor we read 45°. Hence the angle CAD is an angle of 45°.

251C We may use the protractor to construct an angle of any size we wish. 

For example, suppose we wish to construct an angle of 25°. We first draw a line AB. Then we place the protractor with its central point or notch at A so that its straight edge lies along AB, as shown in the diagram above.

Next make a dot at C where the mark on the protractor indicates 25°. Now we remove the protractor and draw a line from A to C. Then the angle CAB is an angle of 25°. 

Draw what looks to you like a right angle. Measure it. 

How nearly correct were you? Try again by estimating angles of 45°, of 30°, of 60°, of 10°. Have another pupil draw several angles with the protractor, then you try to Right Triangle estimate the number of degrees in each of them. Then you may draw the angles and let him estimate the size of each. 

A game like this will help you to get a good idea of sizes of angles. 

Drawing Right Angles. Have you ever seen a carpenter at work? He uses an instrument called a square to draw a right angle. 

Sometimes a celluloid model of a right-angled triangle is used. 

Ask one of your friends, who studies mechanical drawing, to show you how he draws a right angle by the help of his drawing board and T-square. Do you see why this instrument is called a T-square?

DIAGRAM OF A MARINER'S COMPASS 

these are not what we mean when we speak of the points of the compass. The expression points of the compass refers to the directions, north, east, south, and west, which we have just been studying. They are best shown on an instrument called a mariner's (sailor's)

MEASURING DIRECTIONS 

125 compass, which is not at all like the compasses we have used. All ships have mariner's compasses by which they steer their courses. On the opposite page is a picture of one. 

If you know an old sailor, get him to tell you some readings of the compass. If you don't know a sailor, see if you can figure out for yourself the meaning of "north-northeast by north and a half north." Measuring Directions. Whenever it is necessary to state the direction of a line more accurately than is possible by use of the points on the mariner's compass, we state, first, whether the direction is nearer north or south, and second, how many degrees it varies from north or south and whether toward the east or the west. For example, the line with the single arrow points north and is called a meridian. The line with the double arrow points in a direction which is 20 degrees west of north. This is read "north, 20 degrees west," and is written N. 20° W. Also the line with the three arrows points S. 70° E.

This method is always used to give accurately the direction of a line, except when it points exactly east or west. 70° Draw a north and south line and near the center of it mark a point 0. The Circle Graph. The mariner's compass shows a complete circle divided into 360 degrees. Because of these uniform divisions circles are often used as graphs. Car- fare Lunch 20 25¢ Bank Movie 35¢ 20¢ 

One Dollar

Being round like coins, they are often used to show proportionate expenditures. 

Thus if your father gives you a dollar and you spend 20 cents for car fare, 25 for lunch, 35 for a movie, and put the rest in your bank, you would show it graphically as in the figure at the left. 

Let the circle represent the dollar. As 25 cents is one quarter of the dollar, we represent it by of the circle, or 90°. With a protractor we lay off the 90° angle with its vertex at the center. 20 cents is of the dollar, so we use of 360°, or 72°, for the angle opening that shall represent car fare. 35 cents equals, or, of the dollar and 

2 of 360° is 126°, therefore we construct with our protractor an angle of 126°. The rest of the space represents the amount put in the bank.

Parallel Lines. In our circle graphs and study of the points of the compass we learned more about angles. 

But some lines do not form an- C_ gles. Two straight lines on the same plane (flat surface) are called parallel, if they would never meet however far extended.

Parallel lines are the same distance apart at all points. 

The rails of a straight railroad track, the opposite edges of your book, and the edges of the window casing are examples of parallel lines. Look around the room and see how many more examples of parallel lines you can find.

Like right angles, parallel lines are one of the commonest figures of geometry. We are now going to study something in which both right angles and parallel lines are constantly used.

Squared paper which we used to make graphs on page 80 is an illustration of parallel lines and right angles. It is a great help in drawing. In the pocket at the back of the book you found a sample of this kind of paper. Show how you can use it like a ruler for measuring. Draw on the squared paper a right angle ABC. Now draw from B a line BD that will divide the right angle into two equal angles. How can the point D be located? How many

TT degrees are there in each of the angles ABD and DBC? 

Draw a line EF parallel to BC. How did you locate the points E and F? How far apart are the lines EF and BC? Draw EG parallel to AB. What kind of angle is GEF?

Drawing to Scale. Before a building is built the architect draws plans for it. He cannot conveniently make a drawing as large as the floor. If the floor is to be 30 feet wide and 40 feet long and he makes a 4-inch line to represent the length, how long will he make the line which represents the width? Will he make the angles in the drawing right angles as they are in the floor? 

A drawing made like this is said to be drawn to scale. That means that all the angles are just like those in the building to be constructed, but a smaller unit is used for the lines. The drawing is a graph in which the ratio of the lines is the same as in the proposed building.

END-OF-UNIT TRIAL OF I. R. B. TESTS 

In this unit you have made a start on a new branch of mathematics called "geometry." In a former unit you learned how graphs are line pictures, lines of ratios, or fractions. Now you know that geometry also is a study of pictures of all kinds of lines and other figures which lines make. 

You have recorded some geometric terms in your I. R. B. and taken two short tests of everyday addition. Try these tests again in out-of-school time. Enter your second scores (be sure that they are equal to or better than the First Trial scores) in the End-of-Unit column. 

A BASEBALL DIAMOND. HOW DID GEOMETRY HELP TO LAY IT OUT?

KEY THOUGHTS 

Knowledge isn't of much value to anyone if it is not put to use.

Now that you know something about the importance of geometry, what can you do about it? Why, learn to use the facts, of course.

Measurements of all kinds are perfectly familiar to you. 

Yards, miles, feet, inches are well-known terms. You know how easy it is to measure a desk or a picture or lengths of cloth. But how about measuring the height of a church steeple, or a tree, or a tall building? You probably think that is not so easy. But you are wrong. By means of geometry, we can measure great heights and wide distances as easily as lesser ones. Just wait and see.

It is the same with bisecting or dividing a line or an angle. 

Actually it takes very careful measuring with a ruler and other even more accurate instruments to divide a line or an angle into a number of exactly equal parts. But geometry shows us an easy way of doing it. When you want to construct any four-sided figure rectangle, square, parallelogram, or any of the others geometry comes to your aid once more. The ancient 

Egyptians knew well how to use geometry, for the famed pyramids of Egypt stand as an eternal testament of this fact.

Geometry is a first-rate helper to the carpenter, the designer, the architect, and the surveyor. And this next chapter will tell you how it can help you right now. 144

UNIT V  

In the preceding unit we saw how common geometric forms are. We have always known lines, angles, and circles. Now we are going to use them as tools in building up our knowledge of the New Mathematics. 

While we shall learn a new term or two, most of our work will be using what we have already learned. 

Using Geometry to Bisect. "Bisect" means to cut in two in the middle. Of course we can bisect a line by arithmetic, measuring, dividing, and measuring again. Outdoors with a tape this is all right, but for our diagrams and school work and many other practical uses geometry has a shorter way. 

Suppose that we wish to bisect a line segment such as AB. We do not measure the line, but instead, we use the compasses. We open them so that the distance between the points is greater than A- XA the length of AB. Then placing the sharp point of the compasses at one end A, we draw arcs above and below the line. Next, leaving the compasses unchanged, we place the sharp point at the other end B and draw arcs above and below the line, intersecting (cutting) the first arcs at C and D. With the ruler draw the line CD crossing AB at E. You have bisected the line AB at E.

Prove that this is so by measuring. Why must the opening of the compasses be greater than AB? 145

USING COMPASSES TO BISECT LINES 

Draw the lines indicated below and bisect them with the aid of your compasses. Then using your ruler, measure the whole lines and check to see if the halves into which the compasses bisected the lines are actually equal. 

1. The length and width of this book. 

2. A 7-inch line and a 3-inch line.

4. The width and half the length of a sheet of paper.

6. Any line that you may draw. 

7. A line one half as long as the one in example 6.

Another Use of the Compasses. 

In our tennis court diagram we used the ruler to make the service line on a ΔΔ scale representing 21 feet from the net. 

Suppose we wanted to place the other service line without using a ruler. To do this, place the point of the compasses at the bisection (middle) of the net line, letting the pencil point fall at the bisection of the service line. 

Then swing the compasses around to the opposite side and draw an arc across the line bisecting the service court. This measures our 21 feet for the other service line. 

If you wish to mark off on a longer line AB a segment equal to a shorter line XY, first place the sharp point of the compasses on X and open the compasses until the pencil point rests exactly on Y. Then without changing

How to Bisect an Angle. Suppose that we wish to bisect the angle ABC. Here again we may use the compasses instead of the protractor.

Place the sharp point of the compasses on B, the vertex of the angle, and draw an arc cutting BA at M and BC at N. Then with an open- B ing of the compasses greater than

one half the distance from M to N place the sharp point on M and draw an arc. With the same opening, place the point on N and draw another arc crossing the first arc at X. With the ruler draw BX. Then angle ABX angle XBC, and hence the angle ABC is bisected by

the line BX. Prove this with your protractor. Notice that we do not need to know how many degrees there are in the angle ABC in order to bisect it. 

BISECTING ANGLES 

Construct a number of different-sized angles. Bisect them with the compasses; then check up your work by actually measuring them with your protractor. You might use 90°, 45°, 30°, and 180° angles to start with.

How to Construct a Right Angle. All the corners of our tennis court must be square. All the angles must be right angles. Compasses may be used instead of a protractor when we want to make a right angle. We have a line segment

AB, on which we wish to make a right angle at the point C.

This is the same as erecting a perpendicular at the point C, because perpendiculars make right angles.

A X Y B Construct a right angle with compasses and ruler as follows. Place the sharp point of the compasses at C, and swing an arc crossing the line AB at two points X and Y. 

Then open the compasses a distance greater than half of XY, and placing the sharp point on 

X draw an arc. Keeping the same opening, place the sharp point on Y and draw another arc, crossing the first one at M. With the ruler draw 

MC. Then MC is perpendicular to 

AB, and angles MCA and MCB are right angles. Check them with your protractor. If the point C is at the end of the line, we must first extend the line as shown in the figure.

Shorthand Symbols for Angles and Arcs. — One purpose of the New Mathematics is to make our work easier and simpler. So for convenience we use a symbol to stand for the word angle. It is a little angle like this, ZABC. We read this "the angle ABC." Likewise AB is read "the arc AB."

Dropping a Perpendicular from a Point Outside a

Line. Mr. Marsden wanted to make a walk from his front door at right angles to the street. Knowing that

James was studying geometry Mr. Marsden asked him how he thought they could be sure about the angles. 

James drew a diagram showing him how to do it. 

He let the line AB represent the street and the point P the middle of the front door. Then he placed the sharp point of the compasses on P and using an opening long enough, swung an arc cutting AB at two points, C and D. A A-C Next placing the sharp point of the compasses, first on C, then on D, he drew arcs intersecting at M, using the same opening for each. With the ruler he drew PM.

PM was the perpendicular to AB, as he proved by both the protractor and the T-square. 

In geometry we can drop a perpendicular up! Make the point below the line and try it. 

Only one perpendicular can be drawn from a point to a line.

MAKING AN ANGLE EQUAL TO A GIVEN ANGLE 151 

Making an Angle Equal to a Given Angle. In the study of triangles, we not only measure lines, bisect angles, and erect perpendiculars, but we must learn to construct an angle equal to any given angle. This is how we do it. -K Let ABC be the given angle. We take the line HK for one side of our new angle, and use H for its vertex. 

Place the sharp point of the compasses on B and draw an arc cutting the sides of the angle ABC at M and N. 

With the same opening, place the point on H and draw a similar arc cutting HK at D. Set the compasses at N and open them so that the pencil point is at M. Using this opening, place the point at D and draw an arc cutting the other arc at E. Draw EH. Then EHK is equal to the 

LABC. Prove this with your protractor.

CONSTRUCTING AND TESTING ANGLES 

1. With your protractor draw an angle of 35°. Now draw a straight line and with the compasses construct an angle of the same size at some point on the line. 

2. Draw an obtuse angle. Construct with the compasses an angle equal to it. 

3. Test the accuracy of your work by measuring the angles with your protractor. 

4. Draw a line segment, and mark down a point outside of it. Construct a perpendicular line from that point to that line. With your protractor measure the two angles formed. Are they right angles? Are they equal?

Triangle. On a picnic the girls made a table by setting a rickety box on its side. The box had no top or bottom, so it settled and finally broke down. George Burns set it up again and nailed a stick slantwise across the open top so as to make a triangle. This time it held up. 

Can you tell why? 

MAKING THE BOX RIGID 

The beauty of a triangle is that it is stiff and rigid. Steel bridges have to be rigid; they always show many triangles. It is this same rigid or fixed quality which makes triangles so valuable in mathematics, especially for fixing points. We have used triangles in our work. 

PART OF A STEEL BRIDGE 

Courtesy Alaska S. S. Co.

Look for triangles in its construction. 1 A triangle is a plane ¹ figure bounded by three straight lines, like the figure ABC on page 154. 1 ¹ By a plane we mean something smooth and flat like the top of a desk or smooth pond. Can you mention some other examples of planes?

The three lines are called the sides and the points where they meet are called vertices (each one is a vertex). Thus a triangle has how many sides, vertices, and angles? 

Kinds of Triangles. When the sides and angles differ in size, they make different kinds of triangles. 

An acute triangle is a triangle in which all three angles are acute.

An obtuse triangle is a triangle in which one angle is obtuse.

A right triangle is a triangle in which one angle is a right angle.

RIGHT A OBTUSE A ACUTE Δ 

The hypotenuse is the longest side of a right triangle. 

Which angle is it opposite? How many acute angles are there in a right triangle? in an obtuse triangle?

An isosceles triangle is a triangle which has two sides equal. Its base angles are also equal. 

A scalene triangle is a triangle whose sides are all unequal.

HOW TO CONSTRUCT TRIANGLES 

155 An equilateral triangle is a triangle which has all of its sides equal. Equilateral triangles are also equiangular, that is, all three angles are equal. 

How to Construct Triangles. 

We have learned how to draw lines of a definite length and how to make angles of a definite size. Now we are going to put these things together to make different kinds of triangles. a. Constructing a Triangle with Three Given Sides. -If we wish to make a triangle whose sides are equal respectively to three line segments a, b, and c, we first draw a line AX of any convenient length. From A we mark off with the compasses a distance AB equal to the longest segment c. Then we open the compasses a distance equal to the segment b and placing the sharp point at A we swing an arc as shown in the diagram. Finally with 

B as center and a radius equal to the segment a we draw an arc crossing the first arc at the point C. Then we draw the straight lines AC and BC. The triangle ABC has sides equal to the given lines. 

With your ruler measure the line segments a, b, and c, and then measure the sides of the triangle and check the work.

Construct a triangle whose sides are 14 inches, 2 inches, and 24 inches. 

Can you construct a triangle if a + b is smaller than c? 

Why? Would it be possible if a + b just equaled c?

b. Constructing a Triangle When One Side and Two 

Adjoining Angles Are Given (Optional). Suppose you wish to construct a triangle with given a side c and two given angles A' and B'.

B' C. On any convenient line mark off a segment AB equal to Then at A construct an angle equal to the given angle A', as you did on page 149, and at B construct an angle equal to the given angle B'. Extend the sides of these angles until they meet at C. 

Then the triangle ABC contains the given parts. Using your protractor and compasses, check your construction. c. Constructing a Triangle When Two Sides and the 

Angle between Them Are Given (Optional). — Suppose we wish to construct a triangle when we have given the angle A and its two sides b and c. 

On any line mark off from A a line segment equal to c. 

At A construct an angle equal to the given angle A. 

On the other side of this angle mark off a line segment equal to b. Draw CB. Then the triangle ABC contains the given parts. Check this with ruler and protractor.

Finding Lengths, Heights, and Distances by Scale

Drawing. George wondered about the height of the flag- 

60 feet pole in front of his school. 

One day his teacher told him to make this experiment.

George took the blackboard compasses he used in his mathematics class, and going out on the lawn, he placed the compasses at the end of the shadow of the flagpole, so that one leg of the compasses was on the ground and the other pointed to the top of the pole. He tightened the set screw so the compasses would remain at that angle. Afterward he measured the angle, called the angle of elevation of the top of the pole, and found it to be 

32°. He also measured the shadow, which was 60 feet long. Then using squared 32 -90-

There are several kinds of quadrilaterals.

A parallelogram is a quadrilateral in which both pairs of opposite sides are parallel.

A rhombus is an oblique parallelogram whose sides are all equal.

A square is a rectangle whose sides are all equal.

A trapezoid is a quadrilateral with two and only two sides parallel.

The parallel sides of a trapezoid are called bases, and the non-parallel sides are called legs. 

If the legs are equal, it is an isosceles trapezoid.
