KEY THOUGHTS The greatest value that any tool possesses is its capacity to simplify work.

Think, for instance, of all the tools there are to make housework easier. Off hand, there's the vacuum cleaner, the electric stove, the oil-burning furnace.

Think of the tools that have made communication quicker and easier telephone, wireless, radio. 

So it goes.

The human race is always discovering some new way to make work easier. One man invents a new tool. 

Then everybody gladly makes use of it.

Geometry is one of your useful tools that can be used to simplify your work in arithmetic. It has a sister helper, too, called algebra. Algebra is that very handy branch of mathematics that makes use of letters as well as figures to represent numbers. You are going to find out, shortly, just how helpful that can be.

Areas of any plane surfaces, regardless of shape, can be computed very easily by using the facts of geometry and certain algebraic formulas. 

This unit is a challenge to you to learn these ways of simplification. 178

UNIT VI  AND ALGEBRA 

On page 143 is a picture of a baseball field. The bases are 90 feet apart and the base lines meet at right angles. 

This makes a square with dimensions (measurements) of 90 feet by 90 feet. The total space inclosed is the actual size of the figure. Mathematics uses the name area in referring to the size of a figure. The distance around the figure is called the perim'eter. (Peri means around, and meter means measure.) 

Measuring Area. Area is measured by determining how many square units it contains. These square units may be square miles, square rods, square yards, square feet, square inches, square centimeters, etc. A square inch is a square each side of which is one inch. A square centimeter is a square each side of which is one centimeter. See the tables at the back of the book.

This figure contains 6 square centimeters. What kind of figure is it? 

4 yards 

5 yards

Area of a Rec- 

1 1 1 sq. cm. sq. cm. sq. cm.

1 1 1 sq. cm. sq. cm. sq. cm. tangle. — Gerald's mother wished to put linoleum on her kitchen floor, and asked 

Gerald to find out how many square yards it contained. He measured it with a yardstick and found that it was 4 yards wide and 5 yards long. 179

Gerald drew a diagram to scale (page 179), using inch to represent a yard. Then he drew lines parallel to the sides and divided the diagram up into squares, each of which represents a square yard. Gerald counted the squares and told his mother that the area of the floor was 20 square yards. Then his mother told him that he had done more work than was necessary. She said to him: " If you study your drawing, you will find that there are 4 rows of 

5 square yards each, or 4 times 5 square yards equals 

20 square yards."

In other words, the number of yards in length times the number of yards in width equals the number of square yards of area. 

4 yards

Gerald wished to know whether he could always use this method even if the length or width contained fractions, so he measured the pantry floor, which was 4 yards long and 21 yards wide. 

He made a drawing of the floor like the one shown here. 

22 yards 1 1 1 1 1 1 1 222 

How many square yards in this figure? 4 X 2 = ? 

Do these results agree?

We conclude that we can always find the area of any rectangle by the following rule. To find the number of square units in the area of a rectangle, multiply the number of units in the length by the number of like units in the width.

A Formula for Area. When on page 149 we used symbols for angles and arcs, we learned that the New Mathematics is always trying to help us do things in shorter and easier ways. One of the most helpful short-cuts in mathematics is called a formula.

THE FORMULA BRINGS US TO ALGEBRA 

181 A formula is a rule expressed in symbols. If we wish to express the rule above in symbols, we let the symbol stand for the number of units in the length, the symbol w stand for the number of units in the width, and the symbol A stand for the number of square units in the area. 

This gives us the formula A = lw. 

The main thing to remember in formulas is that symbols placed side by side are to be multiplied, not added. 

In this formula lw means I times w. 

The length of a rectangle is usually called its base, and its width is called its altitude. Altitude means the same as height and is usually represented by the letter h. The base is represented by the letter b. So the formula 

A lw may also be written A = bh.

Illustrative Example: Find the area of a rectangle 8 inches long and 5 inches wide.

Solution: 

= 8 times 5

= lw (l times w) 

40 square inches.

Note that we substitute 8 for l and 5 for w, just as one player is substituted for another in football. 

The Formula Brings Us to Algebra. 

In our formulas we saw that A was a symbol for all areas. The letter l is a symbol for any length. This use of letters brings us to another branch of the New Mathematics which uses letters as well as figures to represent numbers. This branch of mathematics we call algebra. 

When you calculate distance, perimeters, areas, volumes, percentage, interest, speed, circumference, and many other things in real life, you will use formulas.

Algebra uses the processes of arithmetic besides some special ones of its own. It uses the same signs: +, X,÷. The most important thing to remember in algebra, as in the case of formulas, is that two letters or letters and figures, when written side by side, are to be multiplied. Thus bh means b times h; 2l means 2 times l; abc means a times b times c. A = 82 

Squares in Algebra. The use of squares is very common in algebra. There is a simple formula for getting the area of a square. If s represents the number of units in the side of the square, then A s X s. 

We do not usually write s times s as ss but write it s² instead. This gives us the formula A = s². 

This is read "A equals s square." Here the 2 is called an exponent. It is a small figure written above and at the right of a letter or number to show how many times the letter or number is to be used as a factor. Here it means that s is multiplied by s, or 

2 s's are multiplied together. 

How much is 22? 32? 52? 62? 8²? 10²?

Illustrative Example: Find the area of a square whose side is 15 inches.

Solution: s2 152 15 X 15 

225 square inches.

Finding the Area of a Triangle by For mula.

In a triangle one side is called the base, and the perpendicular line drawn from the opposite vertex to the base is called the altitude or height of the triangle. 

Harold Mann's father owns a triangular lot at the corner of Park and Center streets.

It has the dimensions shown in the figure. 80 ft. Center 

Park St. 60 ft. 100 ft. 

Mr. Mann's Lot 

He offered Harold a dollar if he could tell him how many square feet しん ​h were in the lot. Harold asked his base teacher, and she gave him this exBase periment to try. Let each try it. 

Draw a triangle and cut it out.

Now use it as a pattern and cut out another like it. Draw the altitude of each. Now place them together as shown in the figure. 

What kind of figure is made by the two triangles? Is its base the same as that of the triangle? Is its altitude the same? The area of the parallelogram is b× h. The triangle is what part of the parallelogram? Then the area of a triangle must be its base times its altitude. 

Harold rightly concluded that the area of a triangle can always be expressed by the formula A = 1 bh.

That afternoon Harold went home and measured the dotted line shown in the figure, being very careful to get it perpendicular to the 100-foot base. He found it to be 

48 feet long. "Then," he said, "the area of the triangle is 1 24 

X 100 X 48 or 2400 square feet." Do you think he earned the dollar?

Jack Wood, when he learned about this experiment, went home and measured the gable to his father's garage. 

He found it was 12 feet long and 

4 feet high. 4 feet 12 1 feet. 

Then he found the area was 3 × 12 × or 27 square feet. How 2' much will it cost to paint the two gables of the garage, each two coats of paint, if each coat costs 30 cents a square yard?

Finding the Circumference of a Circle. An Interesting 

Experiment. The distance around a circle, or its length, is called the circumference. Try the following experiment.

Collect several circular objects, such as a half dollar, a saucer, or an ink bottle. Measure carefully the diameter of each. Cut a narrow strip of paper and wrap it around the edge of the circular object you measure so that the edges of the paper just come together. Measure the length of this strip. This represents the circumference of the object.

To check your work, draw a line on a sheet of paper. 

Now mark a point on the edge of your object, for instance, the saucer. Place this point at one end of your line and roll the saucer along the line, being careful not to let it slip. When it has rolled around once, mark a dot on your line where the point on the saucer falls. Now measure the distance between this dot and the end of your line.

FINDING THE CIRCUMFERENCE OF A CIRCLE 195 

In fact, very careful measurements, as well as other methods of proof which you will study later, have shown that the circumference of any circle is always exactly the same number of times the diameter. 

Alvin Ladd of the Roosevelt school got the results shown in the following table:

OBJECT DIAMETER CIRCUMFERENCE 

C÷D 

Half dollar 13" 16 32" 310% 15 Elec. battery 25" 81" 3100 14 Saucer 6" 187" 31100 100 Piano stool 14" 451" 3100 14 

Tin can 4" 12" 3110% 12 

Ink bottle 2" 61" 316020 We see that the circumference (C) is almost exactly the same number of times the diameter (D) in every case. 

Your own experiments will show a similar result if you are careful.

The circumference of a circle is about 314 or 34 or 

22 times the diameter.

This number is usually represented in formulas by the 

Greek letter π (pronounced pi).

To find the circumference of a circle, multiply its diameter by π (22 or 3.14).

In formula form this rule may be stated С = τd. 

The diameter is equal to twice the radius;

that is, d = 2 r. 

Therefore if we wish, we may write the formula 

C = 2 πr.

FINDING THE AREA OF A CIRCLE 

199 

Finding the Area of a Circle. — Try the interesting area experiment that follows.

Draw a circle on graph paper with a radius of one large square (or ten small squares). Draw two diameters perpendicular to each other. Now draw a large square around the circle, as shown in the diagram. The diameters divide this square into four smaller squares. Each side of one of these squares is the radius of the circle, hence the area of each square is r². 

Therefore the area of the large square drawn around the circle must be 4 r². 

Is the circle as large as this square? ·A = r² A=r2 -A = r² A = r² Then the area of the circle must be less than 4 r². 

To get a clearer idea let us count the number of small squares in r². There are just 100 of them. Now let us count the number in the circle. We find just 284 whole ones and 60 parts of squares. These 60 part squares may be estimated to average half a square each, so that they are equal to 30 whole squares. The circle then contains about 314 small squares and r² contains 100 of them. 

How many r2 is the circle? We find then that the circle is equal to about 

3.14 r². 

What did we call 3.14 in the last section? To what common fraction is it about equal?

This shows us that the formula for the area of a circle is 100)314(3.14 300 140 100 400 400 πr².

This formula means that to find the area of a circle, multiply the radius by itself and the result by π (22 or 3.14). 

Illustrative Example: Find the area of a circle whose radius is 

14 inches. 22 2 × 14 × 14 

616 square inches.

VOLUMES 

Volume. The volume of a solid tells us how much space the solid occupies, or what its capacity is. For example, a quart measuring cup contains twice the volume of a pint cup, though it does not require twice as much tin to make it.

Area. The total area of a solid is its entire surface. 

The amount of paper required to cover a box represents the area of the box.

There are many kinds of solids: cubes, rectangular solids, prisms, cylinders, pyramids and cones, spheres, and more complicated solids which we shall not study. 

Cubes. Anthony Simmons's small brother plays with blocks just as some of your brothers and sisters do. One day his father explained to Anthony that the blocks were cubes. A cube is a solid, bounded by six equal squares. What kind of angles do the edges make with each other?

A cube each of whose edges is one inch in length is called a cubic inch. If the edge is one foot in length, it is called a cubic foot, and so on. We use cubic inches, cubic feet, cubic yards, and the like to measure the volumes of solid figures just as we use inches, feet, and yards to measure the length of lines. Cubes are not always used for measuring, however, and they may have edges of any length. 

How many edges has a cube? How many vertices (corners)? Are all the edges the same length? Volume.

To find the volume of a cube find the cube of its edge. For example, if the edge of a cube is 4 inches, its volume is 43 or 4.4.4 64 cubic inches. If the edge is called e and the volume V, this leads us to the formula V = e³.

RECTANGULAR SOLIDS 

209 

Area. The cube has six sides, each of which is a square. If the edge is 4 inches, each square would contain 42 or 16 square inches, and the 6 sides would contain 6 16 = 96 square inches. A formula for the total area of a cube is S (surface) 6 e². 

PRACTICE WITH CUBES 

1. Find the number of cubic inches in a cube whose edge is 3 inches; 5 inches; inch. 

2. Find the total area of each of the above cubes. 

3. Find the volume and total area of cubes whose edges are 2 feet, 7 inches, 5 yards, 6 centimeters, 8 meters.

Rectangular Solids. Anthony's father built a bin to hold grain. It was 5 feet long, 3 feet wide, and 4 feet deep. Anthony figured that if he had 

cubes each of which was a cubic foot, he could lay just 5 of them in a row along an edge of the bottom. Three of these rows (15 blocks in all) would cover the bottom with a layer of blocks. Four such layers would just fill the bin. Hence the number of cubic feet in the bin was 5 X 3 X 4, or 60. A solid of this shape is called a rectangular solid. 

Volume. From this illustration Anthony saw that the volume was obtained by multiplying 5 X 3 X 4. 

By calling the volume V, the length 7, the width w, and the height h (l, w, and h being the same kind of units) Anthony made this formula V = lwh. 

His sister asked him one day to explain in words what the formula meant. He said,

"To find the number of cubic units in the volume of a rectangular solid, multiply the number of units in the length by the number of like units in width by the number of like units in height." 

She then asked him to show her how he would find the volume of a rectangular solid 12 inches long, 9 inches wide, and 6 inches high. 

He said, "You first write the formula

V = lwh. 

Then you substitute the values in it and Or 

V = 12.9.6. 

V = 648 cubic inches."

Area. The total area or entire surface (not volume) of a rectangular solid, such as the amount of cardboard necessary to make a closed box, can be found by adding the areas of the rectangles which bound it. The base is a rectangle whose area is lw. The upper base (top) is the same. 

Each end is a rectangle whose area is wh. Each side is a rectangle whose area is lh. 

Hence if we denote the entire or total area by S, we have the formula :

S = 2 lw + 2 lh + 2 wh. 

Example: Find the total surface of a rectangular solid 

10 inches long, 8 inches wide, and 5 inches high. = = 

Solution: Herel 10, w 8, and h = 5. Using the formula, we obtain 2.10.82 · 10 ·5 + 2.8.5 160+100+ 80 = 340. 

Therefore the total surface is 340 square inches.

Conserving and Using Our Country's Wealth.- 

Conserve means to preserve or take care of. America has great riches in beautiful forests full of valuable timber and inhabited by game and wild life of all kinds; streams and lakes abounding in fish; fertile and well-watered soil; and the earth full of valuable mineral deposits as well as an immense amount of petroleum.

Some of these have been wasted through carelessness, ignorance, or deliberate waste for private gain. If we are patriots, we must do all in our power by word and example to stop this destruction of our country's resources, and obey all laws passed for their protection.

COMMITTEES OF FOUR 

To work four sets of area and perimeter problems 

Have your Steering Committee, with your teacher as ex-officio member, divide your class into Committees of Four and appoint one chairman for each committee. The chairman of each committee should copy the first set of problems and have each of his committee members copy one of the other three sets. Then on a separate sheet each member, including the chairman, should write the answers to the problems of the set copied. Next the chairman should exchange among his committee the copies of the four sets until each member has written on his separate sheet all 43 answers.

The different chairmen should then exchange answer papers and distribute the exchanged papers to their committee members. 

The chairman of the Steering Committee should read the 43 correct answers while the Committees of Four score the papers, allowing 5 credits for each correct answer. The chairmen should find the average score of the committees whose papers were distributed to them. Finally all average scores should be reported to the Steering Committee, who will decide the winning 

Committee of Four.

KEY THOUGHTS 

A period, as you know, is a very important mark of punctuation. As you have been using it in your English composition, it indicates a full stop the end of a sentence. 

It may, then, seem strange to you that there is a very important use of the period, or dot, in mathematics. Here, it is called a decimal point.

When the decimal point is used it tells something very definite about the number in which it appears. All the digits on the left of the decimal point express a whole number; the digits on the right of the point show an additional part or fraction of the whole number. This part is conveniently reckoned in tenths, hundredths, thousandths, and so on. 

You know how important it is to get the period in the right place in your English composition. If you didn't, you would get very strange sentences. It is even more important to get the decimal point in the right place. That dot has a place value of such importance that, if it is moved, it completely changes the value of the number.

This use of the decimal point to express whole numbers and a fraction, with a denominator of ten or multiple of ten, is one more tool which simplifies problem-solving in arithmetic. 

Practice in the work of the next unit will soon convince you what a first-class tool the decimal point is. You will want to learn to use it quickly and expertly. 228

PART II USING THE NEW MATHEMATICS IN OUR EVERYDAY BUSINESS UNIT VII GETTING READY FOR EVERYDAY BUSINESS. 

DECIMALS The Business Man. When we speak of business men, we often think of Henry Ford or John D. Rockefeller, Jr., or some rich man who owns a large business. But clerks, accountants, electricians, lawyers, doctors, or merchants are also business men. Even boys and girls have a business, whether it is selling papers, helping with the housework, running errands, or going to school. 

Preparing for Business. Most business is done with money. Our American money follows what is called the decimal system, which is based on the number ten and powers of ten. So, to prepare for business we must get acquainted with this system. Like many other things in the New Mathematics it will make your work quicker and easier. This is especially true of decimal fractions, which make our money system so simple. 

Decimals. See the Point! 

See the Point! A decimal is a fraction written like a whole number. As its denominator is always ten, one hundred, one thousand, or the like, this is indicated by a point placed before one or more of the figures. To the left of the point is the whole number (integer), to the right the decimal fraction. Decimals 229

may be changed to fractions to decimals. common fractions and common 

One half is written .50 and one quarter .25, just as you think of a half dollar as 50 cents or a quarter as 25 cents.

Often, decimal fractions offer a simpler way of doing accurate work than common fractions. Simple fractions like,, and ğ are usually easier to use than their decimal equivalents .333+, .125, and .625, but large fractions like and are clumsy, difficult tools to work with when compared with their decimal equivalents .04 and .15. 65 You should be careful to distinguish between whole numbers and decimals, and you should be especially careful when reading them. Watch the decimal point.

Illustrative Examples: 

.5 is read "five tenths." .56 is read "56 hundredths." .567 is read "567 thousandths." .5675 is read "5675 ten-thousandths." 

56.75 is read "56 and 75 hundredths." .03 is read "three and three-fourths hundredths."

COACH AND PLAYER GAME FOR DECIMALS 

Let us try the Coach and Player game with these examples.

The purpose of the game is to help each pupil to develop all possible skill in mathematics. Let pupils who have made good 

I. R. B. records act as coaches for those who need to develop more skill. 

Divide the class evenly between coaches and players. Let your teacher and Steering Committee decide which coach will work with which player. Change coaches and players occasionally. The coach is a helper only; he should show how a mistake was made and let the player correct it. When the coach finds that he too needs help, the Steering Committee will advise. 

Then the player, after the coaching period is finished, may want to work the examples again in out-of-school hours. In athletic games, practice follows coaching. This is the best way for a player to improve his skill. 

If your class and your teacher vote favorably upon continuing the Coach and Player game in this chapter, you may wish to change coaches and players from time to time. But remember that all games are optional.

Multiplication of Decimals is illustrated by the fol lowing: 

Illustrative Examples: 

1. .3 × .5 = 1% × 1% 2. .3 .05 10 X 180 

4. 2.5 X .05 = 

100% = .15. 108% X 18 18 X 180 = 1080 .015. 1888 .0168. 125 

100% = .125.

In each answer above there are as many figures to the right of the decimal point as there are in the multiplier and multiplicand together. 

From these we learn the following rule :

Multiply the numbers in the usual way and then from the right toward the left point off as many decimal places as there are in the multiplicand and multiplier together.

ORAL AND WRITTEN WORK FOR COACH AND PLAYER CONTEST 

In the following exercise of 22 examples let the Steering

Committee and teacher decide which examples should be worked mentally. The player of each couple should then give the oral answers to his coach; the coach should write the answers as given and check them. Then the coach should have the player work on paper each example for which an incorrect answer was given. This should be continued until all answers are correct. 

The first two couples to secure correct answers for all problems which were selected for oral work should choose sides for the written contest; choose couples, not individuals.

In the written contest each coach should work all problems as quickly as possible, so as to be free to assist his player; he should not work the examples for the player. 

When all have finished, the Steering Committee should read the correct answers, while coaches and players correct their own answers and count their number of "rights." The written contest will be won by the side having the larger total of rights.

Division of Decimals. - Decimals may be divided 

(1) by a whole number, or (2) by another decimal. (1) When the divisor is an integer the division is performed as shown below.

Illustrative Example 1: State the quotient in each of the fol lowing: 6 cents ÷ 2 

9 dollars 3 

3 cents 

3 dollars 

24 fifths 

15 tenths 5 .8 ÷ 4 = .2 .21 7 .03 

The above examples illustrate the following rule : 

If a decimal number is divided by an integer, the quotient has the same number of decimal places as the number of places in the dividend.

DIVIDING DECIMALS I. R. B. Test [4]. Enter First Trial scores. Work examples 1-12 for this test.

5. .425 5. 2. .45 ÷ 5.

13. Can you state an easy way of dividing by 10? by 

100? by 1000? by 20? State your easy way. below:

When the divisor is a decimal we divide as shown 

You will observe that 6 ÷ 3 = 60 ÷ 30.

Also 4 ÷ 2 

4020 or 400 200 or 4000 ÷ 2000.

From a study of the above examples you will note that the quotient is the same if we multiply both the dividend and the divisor by 10, 100, or 1000. Now let us work the following:

DIVISION OF DECIMALS 

239 

Illustrative Example 2: .024.6. 

If we can get rid of the decimal in the divisor, we shall have a simple example like those under (1) on the preceding page. So we multiply both the dividend and the divisor by 10. We now have .246. This gives us an example like those in (1) above and our result is .04.

Illustrative Example 3: Divide 4.864 by .16. 

30.4 /16.)4,86.4

Explanation. To get rid of the decimal in the divisor the divisor and dividend are each multiplied by 100, thus moving the decimal point to the right of the 6 in the divisor and making it an integer. The point is moved two places to the right in the dividend. The old decimal points are crossed out. 

The division is then performed as in the division by an integer. 

The quotient has as many decimal places as the number of places that remain in the new dividend. 

The above examples lead us to the following rule: 

To divide by a decimal 

1. Move the decimal point of the divisor to the right end of the divisor. 2. Make a new dividend by moving the decimal point to the right as many places as the decimal point of the divisor has been moved. 

3. Divide as in whole numbers. 4. Point off as many places in the quotient as remain in the new dividend.

Averages in Whole Numbers and in Decimals.— Busi ness men often have to figure averages as on page 26. 

When an average contains a fraction, this is usually changed into a decimal.

Illustrative Example: In the same store the average sales per day during the first week of October was $67; second week $63; third week $65; and the fourth week $58. What were the average sales per day during the four weeks of October? $67+ $63 + $65 + $58 = $253. $253 ÷ 4 = $63.25.

USING LARGE NUMBERS IN ASTRONOMY 

See if you can read this: 189,576,328,000,000. What comes after trillions? Astronomers and other scientists have to use large numbers. 

How many stars do you suppose that you can see on a clear winter night when the moon is not shining? Probably many of you will answer "millions," but you would be wrong. As a matter of fact you could not see more than about 2000 stars at any one time with the unaided eye.

The Use of Aliquot Parts of 10, 100, 1000.- Since 25¢ = $1, $2.50 of $10, and $25 of $100, we may use aliquot parts to make our work quicker and easier. 

Illustrative Example 1: Find the cost of 324 pounds at 25¢. 

324 pounds @ $1 $324 or 

25¢ of $1.

Therefore of $324 $81. Ans. 4)$324 $81 

Illustrative Example 2: Find the cost of 164 articles at $2.50. 

164 articles at $10 = $1640 of $1640 = $410. 

Illustrative Example 3: Find the cost of 1728 tons at $25 a ton.

In this unit you have practiced the interchange of common fractions and decimals; you have used the fundamental operations with decimals, you have observed carefully the proper placing of the decimal point, and you have learned that decimals are tools of everyday use.

Now give yourself, outside of school hours, a test of your skill with decimals. For this purpose try the six I. R. B. tests of this unit. 

Enter your new scores in the End-of-Unit column. Remember that you alone must determine your 100% trustworthiness score.

KEY THOUGHTS There is one more way of simplifying work with fractions that is very useful to know about. It is a process by which you can figure by hundreds. It is called per cent, and it means "rate per hundred," or "number per hundred," or "number of hundredths."

Early peoples had no need for such a method of quick figuring. The business which they carried on was so simple that they could easily deal in fractions as fractions. 

It was, therefore, not until the sixteenth century that extensive trading along the shores of the Mediterranean had made business so complicated that a need was felt for a simpler way of dealing with fractions. 

The use of per cent is just another good example of man's ingenuity in discovering tools for his use that will simplify his work and bring about quicker results. 

After you have worked on this next unit, you will be able to interchange fractions, decimals, and per cents as easily as a juggler keeps three balls in the air at one time. 262

UNIT VIII THE . 

— - 

PERCENTAGE 

Per Cent. Every business man talks in terms of per cent. He buys on a per cent discount. He sells. on a per cent gain. He estimates the per cent cost of rent, wages, fuel, light, taxes, etc.

He gives a per cent increase to his employees. He puts a per cent of his profits into enlarging his business. The banker pays us a certain per cent interest on our bank savings. We talk about a per cent depreciation on our automobile. The merchant thinks about a per cent mark-up on his goods. The words "per cent" are used in all businesses and professions.

Thus the business man talks, thinks, and actually lives in per cents. He will tell you that you should learn how to use per cent. 

He wants only 100 per cent work. 

JOHN D. ROCKEFELLER, JR. 

Do 100 per cent work in your mathematics and you may be sure that the 100 per cent business man will have a place for 100 per cent assistants. 263

Per cent comes from the Latin per centum, meaning by the hundred. So in per cent we figure by hundredths. 

Per cents are simply decimals like those we have just been studying, but they are easier because they are always hundredths. 

Suppose we change each of the fractions, 1, 1, 1, and first to a fraction having 100 as a denominator, and then to a decimal. We have 1212424 55000 100 25 100 .50 .25 75 100 = .75 20 100 = .20 40 100 .40 nu 125 1.25 

Since 50% is only another way of saying 50 hundredths, the decimal .50 is called " 50 per cent." The symbol for per cent is %, so we write 50 per cent as 50%. This sign is shorthand for the words " per cent." In the same way the other fractions of the above may be written 25%, 75%, 20%, 40%, and 125%. 

Likewise : 1 331 3 100 1 16/ 100 1 2 662 1 unit 100 100 100%. .331 331%. .66 / 66,3%. 100 1 121 .163 16%. .12 121%. 200 100 8 100 .00% (of 1%).

Fractions, decimals, and per cents are three different tools to do the same work in mathematics. Sometimes one tool is easier to use in your work and sometimes another. 

You should learn how to use each tool in order to choose the best tool for each piece of work. Be sure that tools of the same size are exchanged for each other, e.g., 1, .25, 25%.

Remember that an even exchange never makes trouble.

The First Use of a New Tool. 

Finding the Per Cent of a Number. 

Mary's teacher told her that 12% of her class were doing "A" work.

The class consisted of 25 pupils. 

Mary was anxious to find out how many pupils were being marked A. How could she do it?

In our work with fractions we saw that of meant times. 

For example: of 10 apples means X 10 apples or 5 apples.

Similarly of 12 bushels X 12 bushels 

4 bushels. And of 15 dollars 

X 15 dollars = 3 dollars.

In the same way, 12% of the class means .12 times the number in the class or .12 X 25 or 3 pupils. Remember about the decimal point in multiplying decimals.

Finding Percentage by Algebraic Formula. - We usu ally call per cent rate per cent, or simply rate, or fractional part. The number of which we find the per cent is called the base or whole. The result of our work is called percentage or part. We must get clearly in mind the difference between per cent and percentage. Per cent is the rate; percentage is the result or part. 

Illustrative Example: Find 8% of $160. 

8% of $160 .08 X $160 rate $12.80. base percentage 

We have, therefore, the following rule:

To find the percentage, multiply the base by the rate. 

Or more simply percentage = base X rate.

Using initial letters, we have p = b × r, or written algebraically p = br.

277 Finding Amount of Decrease. — A falling off in business or a cut in salary is often figured on a per cent basis. 

Illustrative Example: Mary was told one day that on account of poor business her employer must decrease her present wages of $18 a week by 16%. How much would she then receive? 

16% or of $18 $18-$3 $3, decrease. Or 6)$18 old wage $15, new wage. 3 decrease $15 new wage

HOW TO FIND THE DECREASE

3. 600 decreased by 7% = ?

Finding Rate Per Cent When the Base and the Per centage Are Given. Helen's father, who was a physician, asked her what per cent of the pupils in her class had been vaccinated. Helen knew that 23 of the 25 members of her class had been vaccinated but she did not know how to change this to per cent. Her father asked her what fraction of the class was vaccinated. She replied 

23. Her father then asked her to change this fraction to a decimal. This she did and obtained .92. Then 

Helen knew that 92% of the class had been vaccinated.

More Algebraic Assistance-Rate Per Cent by a 

Formula. — When Helen solved the problem that her father gave her, she divided 23 (the percentage or part) by 25 (the base or whole) and thus obtained .92 or 92% (the rate or fractional part). She was really using this rule: 

To find the rate when the percentage and the base are given, divide the percentage by the base. 

That is,

Or rate percentage base r = Note that we express division by the fraction as well as pb. Thus 30 .3 

30%, or 20 = 4 = 400%.

Finding the Base or Whole. - One day Allan heard his father say that 5% of the men employed in his factory were ill. Later he heard him tell a neighbor that 8 men were ill. Allan thought from this that he could find out how many were employed in the factory. So he said "5% 1 20." If of the number of men is equal to 8, then there must be 20 X 8 or 160 men in the factory. Was he right?

The algebraic or x method is shown in example 2. 

Illustrative Example 1: 18 is 9% of what number? 9% 

18 (or 9 hundredths). 

180 (or 9 hundredths) of some number is 18. 

Too (or 1 hundredth) of that number is 2. .. That number is 100 × 2 = 200. 

Illustrative Example 2: 20% of a number is 16. Find the number. 

Let x = the number Then x = 16 (20% ㅎ​) x = 16 ÷ 16 X 5 = 80 This solution is by algebra. See how much easier it is. 

The New Mathematics aims to simplify all our work.

RETROSPECT 

1. A real estate agent received 3% of the selling price for selling some property. If he received $9000 as his pay, what was the selling price of the property? 

2. What were the sales of a salesman if he received as his pay $846.50, which was 121% of his total sales? 

3. A real estate agent bought some land for $9650. 

If it increased in value 15% at the end of a year when it was sold, how much did he make on it? (Interest is not to be considered.) Had it decreased 25%, for what would he have had to sell it? 

4. Another real estate speculator bought some property for $12,000 and found that it was decreasing in value due to manufacturing interests moving away from that city, so he had to sell it at the end of a year at a decrease of 14%. How much did he lose? Find the selling price.

5. What per cent would Frank receive if out of 50 examples he did 40 right? 30? 45? 42? 

6. A real estate agent received $120 for collecting rents. If he received 5% of all of his collections as his pay, how much rent did he collect? 

7. 22.8 pounds of butter fat were obtained from 480 pounds of milk produced by a very good Jersey cow. 

Find the per cent of butter fat. 

8. A tailor made 80 yards of cloth into suits. If he used, on the average, 6 yards for each suit, how many suits did he make? 

9. What per cent of a rod is 3 feet 8 inches? 

Hint: First change each to feet. 

10. One mile of railroad track was to be laid with rails which weighed 80 pounds to the yard. If 1000 pounds of such rails cost $20.75, how much would it cost to lay the tracks? 

11. A railroad train is running at the rate of 60 miles an hour. How many feet per second is it traveling? 

12. Which is greater and how much greater, 5.8% of $1500 or 4.6% of $2000? What is the ratio of the smaller to larger? 

13. A man invested of his money in real estate, † of the remainder in stocks, and still had $6000 in the bank. 

How much property had he in all? 

14. A merchant had a suit marked $24 before a sale.

During the sale he sold it for $18. How much did he reduce it? What per cent did he reduce it on the marked price? 

15. Mr. Scott received $2600 a year. His employer increased his salary to $2860 the next year. What per cent was he raised? How much per week was he raised?

END-OF-UNIT TRIAL OF I. R. B. TESTS 

In this unit you have begun to study some of the mathematics of business. You have discovered that percentage is a very common tool in business. You have practiced the interchanging of equivalents in fractions, decimals, and per cents. You have developed some skill in selecting the equivalent which is the easiest tool to use. You know how business men find per cent of increase or decrease. You have learned how to use rate per cent, base, and percentage and how to find one when the other two are known. You have used formulas which are the beginnings of algebra. Thus you have learned how the several parts of your New Mathematics coöperate to solve your own and others' everyday problems of business. 

Now take once more the seven I. R. B. tests of this unit. Try to improve your First Trial record. Work independently out-ofschool hours. Record your best time and accuracy scores in the column headed End-of-Unit Trial. Remember the greatest test of all is the trial of your trustworthiness. a 100% record, you need not be disturbed overmuch by lower scores in your mathematics skill. They will improve with practice.

KEY THOUGHTS 

Did you ever have a lemonade-stand in your front yard and sell rather watery lemonade to passers-by for a cent or two cents or maybe five cents a glass? 

If you did, you remember that whatever money you made was clear profit, for your mother, without doubt, furnished the lemons and the sugar. 

But suppose you had bought the lemons and sugar yourself. Then in order to make any money you had to charge enough for each drink so that you would get back all you spent and a little more besides. That little more was your profit.

Anyone who goes into business, whether it is the boy who sells papers on the street corner or the owner of the big grocery store in the square, wants to make money. How much profit he can or ought to make is a problem each business man has to solve.

Now the profit for a big business isn't quite so simple to figure as the profit on your lemonade stand. That is why the next unit is included in this book. In it you will learn just how profit is figured and upon how many things it depends; you will learn some new terms, but you will find that all the transactions mentioned discount, commission, mark-ups, bargains, and all are computed by the old familiar item, per cent. 

300

UNIT IX . 

MARGIN 

The Language of Business. We go into business to make money. To tell how much we make we must know not only costs and selling prices, but also what business men call overhead, or operating expense. Overhead includes rent, salaries, light, heat, telephone, stationery, insurance, and all expenses connected with a busi- These expenses sometimes amount to more than the original cost of articles purchased. ness.

For instance, in a shoe business, if you buy a pair of shoes for $3.00 and sell them for $5.00, you do not make a profit of $2.00. That $2.00 is your margin, the difference between buying and selling price. It is sometimes called gross profit. Your profit is what is left of the margin after a certain amount is deducted for overhead. If your overhead averages $1.20 for each pair of $5.00 shoes sold, your profit is $.80. This is sometimes called net profit.

Per cent of gross profit, or margin, was formerly figured on the cost as a base, but now it is more often computed on the selling price. Merchants wish to know what per cent of the sales price of an article represents gain, so net profit also is usually figured on the selling price as a base. 

When per cent of margin is figured on the cost, it is called "mark-up." 301

Discount. is called discount.

One of the commonest practices in business This is a percentage of the regular or list price that is taken off because the customer pays cash, or buys a large quantity, or for some other reason. Trade discount is the allowance to the wholesaler or retailer to pay him for handling and selling the goods. A chain store might buy a carload of articles and thus obtain a greater discount. Discount is computed just as we computed percentage on page 270 of the preceding chapter. 

In business, discount is often expressed simply by "off." When you say: "How much off for cash?" you mean: "How much discount for cash?" Anything off if I buy a dozen?" means: "Is there any discount if 

I buy a dozen?

Illustrative Example 1: A merchant had a certain suit marked at $30. On account of poor sales he decided to reduce all of his goods 20%. What was the new price of that suit? 

20% or of $30 $30 

$6. Or $6 $24. 

Therefore the new price was $24. 

5)$30 List price 

6 Discount $24 New price.

40% or of $2.00 = $.80, $.80, discount $2.00 $.80= $1.20, selling price.

Hence his new prices were $1.20 and $.90.

Finding the Rate of Discount. Sometimes a business man knows the amount of the discount, but wants to find the rate. This is shown by the following 

Illustrative Example: Henry Welsh bought for $25 a suit that had been marked $30. Find the rate of discount. 

Henry received a discount of $5 on the former marked price. = 16%, rate of discount. 358005 Hence to find the rate of discount we have the following rule: 

1. Subtract the selling price from the marked price to obtain the discount. 

2. Divide the discount by the marked price to obtain the rate of discount.

Finding the Margin of Loss or Decrease. - Discount is a decrease in the selling price. On page 277 we figured the per cent of decrease. In business if the selling price is less than the buying price, there is a margin of loss or decrease. This is figured by decimals. 

Illustrative Example: George bought a sled for $2 which he afterwards sold at a loss of 34%. How much did he lose and for what did he sell it? .34 X $2 $.68, his loss. $2.00 $.68 $1.32, his selling price.

Finding the Margin of Increase or Advance. — On page 274 we studied the per cent of increase. When we know the cost and the per cent of increase, it is easy to find the amount of the margin of advance or increase. 

Illustrative Example: Harry bought a jackknife for 60 cents and sold it at an advance of 15% on the cost. How much did 

Harry receive for the knife? .15 X $.60 $.09, advance in money. $.60 + $.09 = $.69, his selling price.

Finding the Per Cent of Mark-up or Per Cent of Loss on the Cost. If Harry Smith should buy a kite for 

80 cents and sell it for $1.00, he would make 20 cents on the deal. This is his margin. If Harry had no "overhead" expenses, this would also be his profit. 

This difference, if reckoned as a per cent of the cost, is called mark-up. In the case of Harry's kite he made 

20 cents on an investment of 80 cents. He, therefore, made a mark-up of 28 or or 25% on his investment. 

ALL SET FOR A KITE-FLYING CONTEST

PER CENT OF MARK-UP OR OF LOSS ON THE COST 311 

Sometimes dealers are so unfortunate that they have to sell goods for less than cost. They may do this because of a decline in the market price, because the goods have been damaged, because they are out of season, or for some other reason. In this case there is a loss which is also usually computed as a per cent of the cost. 

Illustrative Example: During the War a grocer bought sugar at 12 cents a pound. Soon afterwards the War ended, prices dropped, and he was obliged to sell it at 10 cents a pound. What per cent did he lose on his first cost of the sugar? 

He lost 2 cents on each pound which cost him 12 cents. Hence he lost or or 16% on the cost.

COACH AND PLAYER PRACTICE IN ESTIMATES 

The Steering Committee should divide the class into coaches and players. The committee with the teacher's assistance should decide which of the 20 examples beginning on page 312 are adapted to estimates; examples omitted will be used for another purpose.

Each coach and player should write an estimate for each example selected for estimating. Then the player should work the example on paper and the coach should check the answer; in case of doubt call on the Steering Committee. Score one for the estimate of player or coach of each couple which is nearer to the correct answer. In the same manner the rest of the estimates should be written, worked, checked, and scored. 

At the end of the estimate work the Steering Committee should total the scores made by all coaches and the scores made by all players. Before deciding whether coaches or players win the game the Steering Committee may give the players a handicap.

After the contest the Steering Committee should work on the blackboard the problems not selected for estimating.

PER CENT OF MARGIN ON THE SELLING PRICE 315 Computing Per Cent of Margin on the Selling Price. 

-Most business firms to-day compute their per cent margin on the selling price. For example, an unfinished article costs 20 cents. Work to put it into finished condition for sale and overhead expenses of selling bring the total cost to 90 cents. The selling price is $1.20. 

The margin between the first cost of the unfinished article and the selling price of the finished article is $1.20 - .20 $1.00. But the net profit is $1.20 .90 = $.30. It is customary to compute the gain per cent on the selling price, or in this case r = .30 1 r = 1.20 4 

25%; therefore, the per cent of gain on the selling price is 25%. 

By considering the selling price as the base the merchant knows just what part of the selling price goes to cover first cost, what part covers overhead, and what part represents gain.

Practically all large business houses now compute their per cent of gain on the selling price as shown in the above illustration. Large businesses like chain stores and department stores are run on a small net profit per article or on a small per cent gain on the selling price. 

Overhead expenses are figured on the selling price. Even calculating machines are adapted to these methods of modern business. Thus the practice grows to use the selling price as the basis for computing per cent gain, profits, commissions, overhead expenses, etc. A former practice of using the cost as the basis is being steadily abandoned. 

So to find the margin per cent on the selling price we have the following rule :

Divide the difference between selling price and cost price by the selling price to obtain the per cent of margin.

Bargains. How Business Men Sell Goods on Two or More Discounts from the Marked (List) Price. - 

Sometimes a man offers two or more discounts for different reasons. When business men do this, we can buy at bargain prices. The way in which these successive discounts are taken off, one after the other, is shown in the following: 

Illustrative Example 1: A merchant sold eggs at 40 cents a dozen. He offered a 10% discount from this price if the buyer bought a crate (30 dozen) and an additional discount of 5% for cash. The owner of a lunch room bought a crate for cash. How much did it cost him? 

List price = 40 cents. 

10% of 40 cents $.40 $.04 

5% of 36 cents $.36 $.018 

30 X $.342 $.36. 

4 cents, first discount. $.018, discount for cash. 10)$.40 .04 

20) .36

Or .018 $.342 30 $10.26 $.342, price paid per dozen. $10.26, price per crate. 

Note: When there are two or more discounts to be taken off, we must first take off one discount and then a second discount from what remains, etc.

The discounts may be taken in any order that you may select, and obtain the same result. For example, take 

20% and 10% from $100. 

a. $100 20% of $100 $80. $80 

10% of $80 

20% of $90 $72. $72. b. $100 10% of $100 = $90. $90 

Do not add the discounts together and then take off their sum. 

If you do this in the preceding example you get $100 30% of $100 = $70. 

Remember, One discount at a time!

WHOLESALERS' DISCOUNTS 

When a retail dealer buys goods of a wholesaler, the latter frequently gives one or more discounts on the list prices of the goods and an additional discount for cash. 

Have pupils volunteer to find actual problems of wholesalers' discounts. Pupils individually or in committees may secure these from actual cases. Ask parents or local merchants for actual cases of wholesalers' discounts. Get all the facts - what the list price was, what quantity was purchased, whether cash was paid, what discounts were given, and what the actual cost was. Make a problem of each case and bring the problem to your next class period.

When the class completes these original problems, have the Steering Committee appoint one pupil as a retail merchant and two pupils as traveling salesmen representing two wholesale houses. Let these three pupils conduct a buying and selling transaction of one of the problems selected by the Steering 

Committee for this purpose.

The merchant should give each salesman the bill of goods which he desires to purchase. The salesmen should offer their discounts and terms of payment, and describe quality and manufacture of goods. The class should determine which of the quotations is the lower.

The merchant should decide which goods to buy; he should base his decision upon discounts, terms of payment, and quality of goods. Repeat the dramatization for another original problem if the class wishes and time permits.

Illustrative Example: By paying cash for coal I received a discount of 10%, and so was able to buy coal for $10.80 a ton. To find what the list price was, I calculated it as shown below.

90% of the gross price, we use the formula on page 282, b and divide $10.80 by .90. 12.

Hence the list price was $12 a ton. 

We have the following rule:

To find the list or marked price divide the selling price by the result of subtracting the rate of discount from 100%.

Selling on Commission.

Many people make their living by selling goods for some other person. Often they are paid a certain per cent of the selling price of the goods that they sell. The money which they receive for selling the goods is called commission. 

Many people who sell butter, eggs, and produce of various kinds do business on commission. So do traveling salesmen, such as book agents.

Some stores pay their sales people a certain wage and then an additional commission on their sales. 

Illustrative Example: A real estate agent sold a house for $12,000. The agent received 5% commission for selling the house. How much commission did he receive? 

5% of $12,000 .05 × $12,000 = $600, commission. $12,000 - $600 $11,400. Net proceeds. Note. The rate that such an agent would receive would vary in different cities and towns from 2% upwards.

END-OF-UNIT TRIAL OF I. R. B. TESTS 

In Unit IX you have been doing the work so common to business men and to all of us who without exception deal with them. You must begin to realize how important it is that you be able to use intelligently and easily the simple business methods involved in the per cent of loss or gain, rate of loss or gain, selling price, overhead, margin, net profit, discounts, successive discounts, rate of discount, marked price, etc. 

Test your skill in using some of these business methods by taking again the four short I. R. B. tests of this unit. Remember: independent work, improved scores, the I. R. B. entries, and your 100% record.

KEY THOUGHTS 

If you had gone into the lemonade business as a serious venture, you would have found it cheaper to buy your sugar and lemons and paper cups in large quantities. If you didn't have enough money to make your first purchases, you might have borrowed from your mother or father:

If your borrowing was on a strictly business basis, you would plan to pay back the money as soon as you could, from your profits. When you did pay it back, the chances are you paid back just the amount you borrowed; no more, no less.

It happens that many business men find it advantageous to borrow money from time to time. They go to a bank to make the arrangements, and, if they are able to satisfy the men in charge as to their responsibility, they are able to borrow what they need.

But the business man, when he pays back the bank, has to pay back more than he borrowed, for the bank charges him for its services. This extra that he pays is called interest. 

Does the business man know how much the extra will be? 

Yes, because money is loaned at a stated rate of interest per year. And, using a tool with which you are already familiar, the borrower can easily figure the amount he will have to pay.

The tool? Per cent. By means of per cent the cost of borrowing any amount for any length of time at any given rate can be computed, and easily, too. 

This next unit will show you just how valuable this per cent tool has turned out to be. 336

UNIT X 

THE FOUNDATION OF BANKING BUSINESS. 

INTEREST MAKE YOUR MONEY WORK FOR YOU 

Interest. In the first three units of our business work, beginning with Part II of this book, we studied decimals so as to master percentage, and then we applied percentage to a number of business problems. We are now to take up another application of percentage used by everybody who lends or borrows money, or who puts money in the bank. 

BORROWING MONEY

PAYING INTEREST 

Here is Joe borrowing a dollar of Alec. In the next picture Joe is paying Alec back the dollar, with five cents extra for the use of it. This five cents is the interest. 

Interest is money paid for the use of money. 337

THE FOUNDATION OF BANKING BUSINESS 

If we live in a house belonging to somebody else, we have to pay rent for the use of it. We pay for the use of an automobile when we hire it at a garage. Similarly, it is right for us to pay interest when we use another person's money. The borrowed money is called the principal. The sum of the principal and interest is called the amount. 

Finding Interest for a Whole Number of Years. — To find the interest for one year you should multiply the principal by the rate. 

Illustrative Example: Find the interest on $650 for 1 year at 6%. $650 principal .06 rate $39.00 interest for 1 year

The interest for 2 years is twice as much as the interest for 

1 year. Thus the interest on $650 for 2 years at 6% is 2 X $39 $78. For 3 years it is 3 × $39 $117, etc.

The amount is the money due the lender, that is, it is the sum of the principal and interest. The amount of $650 on interest for 2 years at 6% is $650 + $78 $728.

Finding Interest for a Fraction of a Year. Three months is of a year, hence the interest on a given sum for three months is of the yearly interest. 

Illustrative Example: Find the interest on $375 for 3 months at 4%. .04 X $375 = $15.00 interest for 1 year. × $15 $3.75 interest for 3 months.

Illustrative Example 2: Henry borrowed $1200, April 4, 1929, and repaid it with interest at 6%, June 16, 1933. How much did he have to pay? 

From April 4, 1929, to April 4, 1933, is 4 years. 

From April 4, 1933, to June 4, 1933, is 2 months. 

From June 4, 1933, to June 16, 1933, is 12 days.

Hence the money was on interest for 4 years 2 months 12 days.

EXACT INTEREST FOR TIME LESS THAN ONE YEAR 343 

Exact Interest for Time Less than One Year (Optional).

Exact Interest is sometimes computed. The United 

States Government uses this method. By this method the exact number of days is found from the time when the money is borrowed until it is paid. Then the interest is calculated for that number of days, using 365 days to the year instead of 360 days.

Illustrative Example: Find the exact interest on $720 from 

August 4, 1932, to March 8, 1933, at 6%.

By the method explained on page 334 there are 216 days. .06 X $720 = 216 $43.20 365 1 $43.20 interest for 365 days $25.56 exact interest for 216 days.

THE FOUNDATION OF BANKING BUSINESS How a Formula of Algebra Helps to Compute Interest. 

-We have had enough examples to find out that the process for working interest examples is always the same. 

Whatever short-cuts we may use, we always multiply the principal by the rate and this result by the time in years. 

This may be expressed by the formula. 

Interest = principal X rate X time (in years)

I = prt. 

Or more briefly

Illustrative Example 1: We wish to use this formula to find the interest on $640 for 4 years at 6%.

Interest

r t $640 X .06 X 4 = $153.60 

Illustrative Example 2: Find by the formula the interest on 

$1800 for 2 years, 4 months at 4%. 

I = $1800 X .04 X 2 $168 

Illustrative Example 3: Find the interest on $720 for 8 days at 5%.

I = $720 X 380 X 180 $0.80 

Using the formula from algebra, find the interest on each of the following: 

1. $630 for 5 years at 6%.

6. $2400 for 6 months at 6%.

8. $1625.50 for 6 months, 12 days at 6%.

COMPOUND INTEREST 

345 Compound Interest. Savings banks pay a form of interest called compound interest in which interest is paid on accumulated interest as well as on the original principal. Most savings banks compute the interest and add it to the principal either semiannually or quarterly. 

When Mary Ellen Tracy was a little girl, her father decided to start a savings account so that when Mary Ellen grew up he would have money to send her to college. He began by depositing on July 1 $200 in the 

Union Savings Bank, which paid 4% interest, compounded semiannually, on January 1 and July 1 of each year. Interest at an annual rate of 4% is the same as 2% for six months. Hence, the following January 1, interest of $4 was due (.02 X $200 $4). This was added to the original principal, so that Mr. Tracy now had $204 in the bank. On the following July 1, interest of $4.08 was due (.02 $204 $4.08). The principal now became $208.08. Cents are disregarded in computing bank interest, so the interest due the following January 1 was $4.16, making the new principal $212.24. This is the way Mr. Tracy's bank book looked after three years. Check each entry to see that it is correct.

Compound interest is more than simple interest because when compound interest is paid, interest is paid on interest as well as on the principal. For example, in 5 years $100 amounts by the above rule for compound interest to $121.84, but by simple interest to only $120. Verify these figures. 

Compound interest on ordinary loans is prohibited by law in many states, but if offered and accepted is not regarded as an exorbitant rate.

FINDING THE REAL COST OF USING AN AUTOMOBILE 

1. Mr. Newton bought an automobile for $1200 and sold it for $500 3 years later. What did his car lose in value in the 3 years? This is known as depreciation. 

2. How much interest did Mr. Newton lose by not lending the $1200 at 5% per year simple interest for the 

3 years instead of buying that car? 

3. He had the following expenses on his car: license for each year $12 for the car; $.50 for a driver's license each year; garage, $10 a month; insurance, $40 a year; new tires and tubes, $48.50; other expenses, $150. Find his total expenses for the 3 years. 

4. Suppose he used 1500 gallons of gasoline at an average of 15.4 cents a gallon and 240 quarts of oil at 25 cents a quart. Find the cost of his gasoline and oil. 

5. Find the total cost of all items of expense in the first four examples. 

6. If he drove his car 22,652 miles, how much did it cost him per mile (to the nearest tenth of a cent)? 

7. If he and one friend used this car, how much is that per mile per person? 

8. If a railroad charges an average of 14 cents a mile for a monthly commutation ticket, how much would these men have saved in the 3 years had they used the railroad for the same mileage instead of the automobile? 

9. Mr. Alvord bought a car for $1080, kept it for 3 years, and then sold it for $320. During the time he owned it, he drove it 42,450 miles and used 3000 gallons of gasoline at an average cost of 16.5¢ a gallon. He paid $4 a month for garaging it, bought 3 new tires at $14.50 each, spent $161.40 for oil, greasing, and repairs. Licenses cost $17.50 a year. What did it cost for 3 years? How much per mile?

THE FOUR FUNDAMENTAL PROCESSES BY TEAM WORK 

Steering Committee and Teacher appoint four team captains; these in turn choose teams. Steering Committee set standard time for Parts 2, 3, and 4 which with standard time for Part 1 should be entered in each I. R. B.

All teams work each part at the same time; write answers only. When all teams have completed Part 1 begin on Part 2 and so on; write all answers on one sheet. Each pupil keep record of his own time for each part.

When Part 4 is finished captains collect papers, exchange, and distribute to teams. Chairman of Steering Committee read correct answers while team members check and score papers; 

Steering Committee explain how to score each Part. Each pupil preserve his own accuracy score. Captains average team scores for teams whose papers were received in exchange; average score for each Part. blackboard graphs of the 

These graphs will show the 

Steering Committee draw on average scores of the four teams. winning team of each part. Can you also find from the graphs in which of the four fundamental processes your class needs continued drill?

Banks. We have just spoken of saving and investing. Both are helped by banks. Banks are the most important institutions in the business world. Without them modern business could not exist. So we must learn about them.

Many towns have school banks where the pupils may deposit their savings, but whether banks are large or small they all have certain things in common.

Samuel Grogan teller at the bank. This is called a blank indorsement. 

If Mr. Grogan deposits the check to his own account, he indorses it in blank.

How Payments Are Made by Mail. 

Cash should not be sent by mail because it may be stolen or lost. Loose silver is likely to wear a hole through the envelope. If the sender has a bank account, money may best be sent by check. 

Other methods of paying bills are by 

Postal money order 

Express money order 

Bank drafts 

Telegraphic money order 

Postal money orders are very handy for paying small bills at a distance. The sender fills out a blank like that shown here.

THE FOUNDATION OF BANKING BUSINESS 

The sender then pays his home post office the amount of the money to be sent plus the fee for the money order. 

This money order is sent to the one to whom the bill is to be paid. The receiver takes it to his own post office when he gets it and there receives the full amount of the money order. He may indorse the money order over to his own bank if he has a bank account. 

The express and telegraph companies issue money orders much as the post office does. The money order of a bank is called a bank draft.

THE FOUNDATION OF BANKING BUSINESS LEARNING TO BE A GOOD CITIZEN 

The good citizen — 1. Pays taxes promptly and willingly for the support of his government. 

2. Obeys the laws cheerfully. 

3. Takes part in elections by voting for a candidate. 

Money raised by taxation is used to pay the salaries of public officials, to build roads, to maintain schools, to provide police and fire protection, to look after the public health, to provide a pure water supply, to light and pave the streets, and for many other purposes. Many people would not care to live in a community which did not provide these advantages.

PRACTICE WITH GOOD-CITIZENSHIP PROBLEMS 

1. A village needed to raise $50,000 to support the schools, $8000 for the streets, $15,000 for police protection, $8250 to maintain the fire department, $12,500 for salaries, $7800 for lighting, and $17,600 for all other purposes. What was the whole amount of taxes to be raised?

Apportion the costs graphically. 

2. Mr. Wallingford, a wealthy man, lived in the above village. He owned 3% of all the taxable property in that village. How much tax would he pay? 

3. In one year the state of New York spent $369,- 

035,300 for its schools. The average daily attendance of pupils was 1,911,215. How much did it cost the state to give a year of schooling to each of these boys and girls? At that rate, what would your education cost if you attended the public schools for 12 years?

4. In one great American city in one year, 465,576 persons were arrested for violating some one of the laws. 

The cost of maintaining the police department of that city for that year was $60,198,591. What was the average amount each law-breaker cost the city in expense of the police department? Mention other expenses to the taxpayers caused by these law-breakers.

Smith 15,016,443; Norman Thomas 267,420; Other candidates received a total of 48,099 votes. Did Mr.

Hoover have a majority? (A majority means more than half of all the votes cast.) Does a candidate for office have to have a majority in order to be elected? What was Mr. Hoover's plurality over Mr. Smith? 

6. In 1932, Mr. Franklin D. Roosevelt received 

22,521,525 votes for President, Mr. Hoover 15,957,537, Mr. Thomas 728,860, and all others 279,348. What was the total vote cast? What was Mr. Roosevelt's plurality over Mr. Hoover? What per cent of the total vote did he receive? 

7. Alfred's class in junior high school had an election for president in which 380 boys and girls voted. They decided that the winner must have a majority. Alfred received 160 votes, Jack 150 votes, and Horace 70 votes. 

Was any one elected and if so whom? What was Alfred's plurality over Jack? After that, Horace withdrew, and the pupils all voted for Alfred or Jack. This time Alfred received 204 votes and Jack 176. Who was elected? 

What was his majority?

Tom Grouch refused to attend the class meeting or take part in the election. Do you think he was loyal?

In this unit you have learned that the foundation of the banking business is interest. You have had practice in some common banking customs. At the same time you have discovered how important to every one of us who deals with banks is skill in the fundamental operations of mathematics. You cannot afford to make mistakes with your own money or with any one else's funds.

Now test your skill with the End-of-Unit Trial of the six tests of 

Unit X. Remember: out-of-school-time; improved record for each trial; 100% honor record in your work.

KEY THOUGHTS 

On those many occasions when you have had to sell tickets for a play, a concert, a dance, or a game, how have you kept track of those who bought tickets, those who paid for them, those who owed for them, and those who promised to buy later?

Now and then, when your family have urged you to keep a cash account and you finally gave in to keep peace, how did you do it?

If you have ever been treasurer of your class or of some club or society, how did you know how much money you had, where it came from, how much you spent, for what it was spent, what bills were paid, and what bills unpaid? 

A record of some kind or another is the answer to all three of these questions. Records are a very important part of all financial transactions. Receipts for money paid, bills for money owed, statements for goods sold, notes for promises to pay, and ledgers for cash accounts are only a few of the forms that are used as business records. 

If you have never had occasion to use any of these forms in your own dealings with others, you are going to have an opportunity now to learn something about them. 

If you have used some of them, you are going to discover just how helpful they are in big business as well as in small. 

For it is on business papers and business forms that the story of business is written. 368

UNIT XI EVERYDAY BUSINESS PAPERS AND ACCOUNTS 

Now that we have laid the foundations of business with decimals, percentage, and interest, we are ready for more practice with other tools of business. We shall run across a new term or two, but all the work will be simply a further application of the New Mathematics which we have already learned. 

Bills. Invoices. Statements. Whenever we buy without paying cash, we usually get a bill for our purchase. Merchants, contractors, and others who do business usually send out bills to their customers at stated times. 

Most wholesalers give a discount (see pages 302-307) as shown in the following bill.

JERSEY CITY, N. J., Apr. 15, 1938 

Mr. S. Barabas 

53 Beacon Ave., City 

Bought of H. WILLIAMS & CO. 

TERMS: Net 30 days, 2% 10 days. 1 doz. 

V. S. P. Sweaters @ 10.00 120 00 

25% 30 90 00 369

EVERYDAY BUSINESS PAPERS AND ACCOUNTS 

99 Bills are of various forms, but they always give the names of buyer and seller, the amount due, and the date. 

Sometimes the terms and the discount for cash are also given. "30 days net means that the bill may be paid any time within 30 days. "2% 10 days" means that 2% may be deducted if the bill is paid within ten days.

Invoices are lists. When the wholesaler sends to the retailer a list of items that the latter buys, together with the price and the discount that are offered, this list is called an invoice of the goods. An invoice is a list, not necessarily a request to pay. On the other hand, a bill is a request to pay, and it may or may not contain a list of items.¹

Statements are bills, sent out monthly, giving the dates and amounts of the month's bills and their total. 

MAKING OUT BILLS 

Make out bills for each of the following: 

Write a check to pay each bill. (Use any names you wish.) 

1. 2 dozen chairs at $50 a dozen; 3 dozen chairs at $30 a dozen; 3 single cots at $15; less 20%. 

2. 3 barrels of sugar, 640 pounds @ 41¢ a pound; 2 barrels of flour, 392 pounds @ 5¢; 453 pounds of tea @ 45¢; 

82 packages of shredded wheat @ 12; less 2%. 

3. 4 pianos @ $460; 2 pianos @ $375; 5 pianos @ $320; less 33%, 25%, and 5% for cash. 

4. 1 dozen baseballs @ $24 a dozen; 11 sweaters @ $15; 6 football head guards @ $6; less 10%. 

1 Look up bill in the dictionary. Because it has so many different meanings business men commonly use the word invoice for it.

RECEIPTS AND VOUCHERS 

371 Receipts and Vouchers. When a bill is paid, the one who receives the money should give the payer a receipt. 

This is usually written on the bill. Below is a receipt for a bill previously rendered. Mr. Somers paid the bill August 20. Mr. Sullivan then returned the bill to 

Mr. Somers with a receipt signed at the bottom as you see. The bill is now called a receipted bill or simply a receipt.

HAROLD T. SULLIVAN CONTRACTOR AND BUILDER 186 LASALLE ST., MILWAUKEE, WIS. 

Mr. Benjamin Somers 

Auburndale, Wis. 

TERMS: 30 days net. 

Aug. 12, 1938 

To building garage as per agreement 

Received payment, Aug. 20, 1938 

Harold T. Sullivan Dr. $425 00 On this bill the expression "30 days net" means that no interest will be charged if the bill is paid within 30 days. 

The abbreviation "Dr." stands for "Debtor," and means that the amount stated under it is owed. 

If Mr. Somers pays this bill by check, he has three evidences that he has paid it : 

1. The stub entry in his check book. 

2. The receipted bill. 

3. The canceled check, which the bank returns to him.

EVERYDAY BUSINESS PAPERS AND ACCOUNTS 

A canceled check or a receipt, in fact any paper which proves a payment, is called a voucher. But in usual practice canceled checks are called vouchers, and receipts are called merely receipts. 

When a business man receives a check in payment of a bill, he usually sends no receipt, as the canceled check when returned by the bank shows that the money has been paid. Thus a canceled check is a voucher, a kind of receipt.

There is another form of receipt, used when no bill is made out (rendered). Here is a receipt for insurance, where no bill was rendered. Received from $36.00

EXETER, N. H., April 4, 1938 

Alex Murphy

Thirty-six dollars for payment of insurance in full 

Millard S. Reynolds.

The receipt states the amount paid both in words and in figures, and usually also the purpose for which the money is paid. In the case of payments made in currency, that is, not by check, it is advisable to have a receipt like the one shown above. 

If you pay a part of a bill, then the receipt states that a certain amount has been received on account.

At the close of all transactions the class should decide to whom each paper belongs.

4. Gerald Martin owed Harry Burgin $150. He had owed this bill for 1 year from June 2, 1937. Mr. Burgin's store charged interest at the rate of 6% per year on all bills that were not paid within 30 days. Find the amount of money that Mr. Martin would owe Mr. Burgin on 

June 2, 1938. See if you can write the kind of receipt that you would like to have if you were Mr. Martin. 

Promissory Notes. Sometimes a man has not the money to pay for goods he wants to buy. If the seller does not wish to lose the sale, he offers the goods to the purchaser on condition that the latter will promise to pay him later. For instance, in the winter or spring farmers often need tools, feed, or seed for which they cannot pay till the crops are harvested in the fall. Merchants sell goods in the spring on the farmer's promise to pay when the crops are marketed. This promise when put in writing is called a promissory note, or just a note.

A note is both an acknowledgment of the debt and a promise to pay. It should state the date, the time it is to run, and the rate of interest. The following is the form of a promissory note.

INDORSING NOTES 

375 

Factoryville, Pa., ....May 15, 1938.... 

I........ promise to pay $....300.00.... 

Three months.......... after date 

Henry Sellers, or order.. 

Three Hundred.. .Dollars

For value received, with interest at six per cent. 

Henry Sullivan.....

The person to whom the money is to be paid is called the payee. Who is the payee in the above note?

The one who is to pay the money is called the maker. 

Who is the maker? What rate of interest must the maker pay? 

Upon what date is the note due?

How much will be due Mr. Sellers, including interest, when the note falls due? 

Indorsing Notes. In the transaction above when the note falls due, Mr. Sellers may desire to have Mr. 

Sullivan pay it to some person other than himself. Suppose that he owes Thomas Crane $500, and wishes this note paid to 

Mr. Crane as part payment. In that case he writes an indorsement on the back like this. 

Pay to 

Thomas Crane 

Henry Sellers

This indorsement makes Mr. Sellers liable for payment of the note. That is, when the note falls due, if Mr. Sullivan fails to pay it, Mr. Sellers must. For this reason we should be cautious about indorsing notes. 

Mr. Sellers must not indorse the note to Mr. Crane, unless it bears the words "or order," or "to the order

EVERYDAY BUSINESS PAPERS AND ACCOUNTS 

of." These words make it negotiable, that is, payable to the person to whom the payee indorses it. If these words are omitted, the note is not negotiable. 

If nothing is said about interest, in most states the note will bear interest at the legal rate. If there is to be no interest, the words "without interest" should be written in the note. 

A note is often called an I. O. U. Can you tell why? 

Who should have possession of a note until it is paid?

HOW MONEY IS BORROWED AT A BANK 377 

How Money Is Borrowed at a Bank. — Banks lend out, at interest, part of the money deposited in them. 

The law limits the rate of interest in different states. 

Bank notes are usually made for 3 months or less. One reason for this is custom with national and other banks, and another reason is that the banks which belong to the Federal Reserve System (which you will learn more about later) cannot pass the note on to the Federal Reserve as security for a loan if it is made for more than 90 days. 

Mr. Peak wished to buy $400 worth of men's hats to sell in the spring season. He does not have the money so he borrows it from his bank for 60 days. He makes out a bank note as shown below. $....400.... 

Sixty days.. 

Delhi, N. Y. ....Jan. 15, 1939.... after date ............. promise to pay to the order of 

Second National Bank.. 

Four hundred 00 100Dollars 

Arthur E. Peak 

Value received. 

No.....156... Due ....Mar. 16, 1939....

The face of the above note is the amount that he borrows, namely $400. 

Mr. Peak may deposit bonds, stocks, or similar material of value with the bank as security known as collateral. 

Or he might have to find some other person of good financial standing in that community to indorse his note as security. In the latter case it is known as a two-name note. Could there be a three-name note? Why? 1

EVERYDAY BUSINESS PAPERS AND ACCOUNTS 

In this note the interest which the bank collects in advance is known as bank discount. That is, on January 15, when Mr. Peak borrowed this money, he had to pay the bank $4, the interest on $400 for 60 days @ 6%. Then the bank would pay him or credit him with the $400 minus $4 or $396, called the proceeds, and at the end of the 60 days, March 16, called the date of maturity, or due date, he must pay the bank the $400 (the face value of the note).

Accounts. We have now become familiar enough with bills, receipts, checks, vouchers, and notes, so that we ought to be able to put them to work in everyday practice. This can be done best by keeping accounts. 

While in school you will be busy with your personal accounts and in raising and spending class and school funds, in school savings accounts, and in many other relations with the business world. People will respect you more if you convince them that a junior high school pupil knows business customs well enough so that he can use the conveniences of the commercial world. 

In the very first unit Jack Wood kept a cash account. 

A few pages back we opened an account at a bank. We are now going to study some other accounts.

At the end of every week Mabel adds the receipt column. Then she adds the expense column, and by subtracting finds out how much money she ought to have on hand. Then she counts her money, and if the results agree, her accounts balance. Then she enters this amount under the expense column and draws the line as shown. 

What is the total of each of the columns? 

If the accounts balance, these columns must agree. 

A real bookkeeper would write the word balance in red.

Now she is ready to open the account for the next week by entering $3.06, the amount of last week's balance, as cash on hand.
